BERYL'S HUSBAND.

"Your silence is sufficient answer," said Miss Cavanagh. "He is not your cousin. You told me that you were poor and friendless. I took yot in out of pity and kindness, and how am I re warded? You told me that you came from schoo at Vevay, and must earn your own living. Sure, I thought it was next to an angel you were. Bu' one day when I was out, comes this young man tc see you as bold as brass. Does that look like friendlessness ? And you told me never a word of your cousin.' it's mighty careful you've been not to mention his name to me since. And one evening you went out and returned home very late in a cab, and you never gave me an explanation of that either. Why should girls of your age receive gentlemen visitors and go out at evening alone? It's sore wounded and troubled I am. I feel that I have been cheated and deceived. It's not of myself I'm thinking, nor of my house. You will go await and never mind me at all; but I shall think often and often of the girl I liked and took into my house, but who turned out badly. Oh, Miss Star, why are you not as innocent and good as you seem ?" The warm-hearted Irish woman choked a little, and put her handkerchief to her eyes. "Are you really my friend, Miss Cavanagh ?" asked Beryl. "I am ; indeed, and I am. And if you'll say to me now that it's all right, I'll believe in you still," said the landlady. " I came to tell you that I must have your room, but sure you can give me an explanation. IV hen I look into your face I cannot believe evil of you." "I am worthy of your esteem, Miss Cavanagh," said, Beryl, gravely. "I need a friend. I should like to confide in you, and believe you will never betray my confidence." "Never. If I find—but of course, you are worthy of my recommendation, Miss Star; I can get some sort of situation for you perhaps," said Miss Cavanagh, brightening. "I thought of you to-day when 1 was at Hawkhurst House—an earl's house, my dear—in dear—in Park Lane—" "Hawkhurst House?"

BY MRS. HARRIET LEWIS, AUTHOR OF Darkwood, " "Lady Kildare," "Sundered Hearts," "Her Double Life," Bto.

CHAPTER XLH. A STARTLING DISCOVERY. On leaving Hawkhurst House, Beryl returned to her lodgings in Bayswater, and did not emerge from her room again that day. Her work was finished on the morning of the fourth day, and it became necessary to dispose of it. There was no use in working elaborate pieces of embroidery unless a market could be obtained for them. Beryl shrank from going from shop to shop, as she had done before, but there was no help for it. She put on her black garments, her hat and short black crepe veil, which effectually shrouded her features, and, work in hand, set forth upon her mission. She shrank from going into the neighbourhood of Oxford and Regent-streets, but that was the fashionable quarter; it was there that wealthy ladies went to purchase costly wares, and it was there she would stand most chance to sell her embroideries. She wandered into Picea.

-dilly, and into the Burlington Arcade. • She soon came out of the Arcade considerably disheartened, and brushed against her old friend Mrs. Margie. In spite of Beryl's black veil, Mrs. Margie knew her. She stood a moment as if stupified, but as Beryl essayed to pass on, the lodging-house keeper clutched her garments, crying out: " Oh, Miss Star, wherever have you been all this time, and your husband wild about you? My dear, oh, my dear—"

Curious eyes were upon them. Beryl came to a halt lest she should create a scene. "How do you do, Mrs. Margie?" she said, quietly. "Come out into the street with me, if you wish to speak to me." Mrs. Margie kept a Close hold upon the girl, but went with her into the open street. "I knew you at once," said Mrs. Margie, garrulously, "in spite of your black dress and that thick veil. I knew you by the way you carried your head, by your figure, your walk, and by your whole self, and I was that took aback I can hardly get my breath yet. Oh, Mrs. Star—l mean Mrs. Desmond—wherever have you been V' "I have been quite safe, Mrs. Margie, and very well in health. How is—is Mr. Desmond' . _

" Yes. It's a perfect palace, and that grand that you'd think the people must be happy there as in Paradise. My cousin is lady-house- keeper there. She was a McGinnis of County Clare, and married a Dominick. She is a widow, is Mrs. Dominick, and quite the grand lady with her big gold chains. She treated me to tea out of real china cups as thin as paper, and India sweetmeats, and such delicacies as earls live on. But this earl is not happy, by reason that his health is failing him quite suddenly. He's a big, bold-looking gentleman, with a flaming red beard, and has a temper to match. His far-away cousin and heir lives with him, but I didn't see him. Mrs. Dominick says that the young gentle- man is very handsome and good." "The earl has red hair ? " said Beryl, as Miss Cavanagh paused from sheer breathlessness. "As red as live flames, Miss Star. He looks like a sort of human lion. But he's ill now, though, to be sure, he won't hear the word, and he would ride out to-day, and he roars out at the idea of seeing a doctor. He needs some one to wait upon him, and read to him, and copy his manuscripts, and write his letters, and he won't let his heir nor his heir's wife do it for him. He told Mrs. Dom- inick to find him some honest, well-bred gentle- woman to come in for a few days to serve as nurse and amanuensis, and if you were not too young, now, I should say this was the very place for you. It would lead to employment elsewhere." "Oh, Miss Cavanagh," cried Beryl, excitedly, "it is the place for me ! I must go there! I must !" "But you are too young," said the landlady, drawing back in surprise. " I can disguise myself to appear older. All the secrets of my life are centering about that Louse. Hear me and help me. I can wear a black wig, and darken my face, and wear spectacles, and no one there would know me, not even the earl's heir. Listen I "

Her voice faltered as she made the inquiry. "He is not well. How could he be? He neither eats nor sleeps, as one might say," said Mrs. Margie, reproachfully. "He spends all his time in searching for you. I believe he thinks that you may destroy yourself. He has grown thin and nervous, and starts at every sound. He thinks that he saw you in Wells-street the other night, and no one can persuade him to the contrary. He spends all his evenings at home now a-watching for you. Oh, Mrs. Desmond, you've treated him cruel! What has made all this trouble? Why did you run away from Mr. Desmond on your wedding day? Were you out of your mind at the time? I have thought that you might have had a sudden attack of dementia." "No; it was not that. I did not know it at the time, but I was not free to marry Mr. Desmond. I discovered that I had made a frightful mistake, and I knew no way to rectify it except to go away. I am sorry for Mr. Desmond, but Lam not ills wife, and I can never see him again." " Never ?" "Never !" said Beryl, firmly. "He is free. In time, he may marry some one he will love. I pray that God will pardon me for the blight I have brought upon his life, and make hint forget me." "My dear," said Mrs. Margie, "this is all non- sense. You have got some curious notion in your head, but Mr. Desmond will reason it all out of you in no time. You have treated him cruel in running off from him as you have, but he'll never throw it up to you if you'll go back to him. If you think that your marriage to him is all wrong, why not reason the matter over with him ? He is a well. principled, hononrable young gentleman, and won't let his feelings influence his convictions. Don't set np your opinion against that of the rest of the world, but take advice; counsel with Mr. Des- mond and that lawyer that comes so often to see him." "I know I am right, Mrs. Margie, and I could not bear the pain of seeing Mr. Desmond. lam nothing to him now. You don't know, Mrs. Margie; you can never know—" "I know one thing," said the woman, as Beryl paused "and that is that I am going to take you hack to your husband if I have to hire a policeman to help me. You ar not in your right mind, Mrs. Desmond; and whether you are or not, your hus- band is your best protector." She clutched Beryl yet more closely, and looked up and down the street for an empty cab. They were in Regent-street now, in the midst of the throng of the promenaders, and did not attract any special attention as they walked on with the crowd. Suddenly Mrs. Margie came to an abrupt- halt, uttering an exclamation of astonishment. . .

In a quick, impassioned voice, every word ring- ing with truth and honesty, Beryl poured forth her own and her mother's story. She knew that she could trust Miss Cavanagh, and she held nothing back, telling of hee birth, her education, her first and second marriages, and all the more recent events, including her discovery of her father that day. Miss Cavanagh interrupted her now and then, put a question or two, but believed the story implicitly from first to last. " I have only one object in life now," said Beryl, in conclusion, " and that is to clear, if possible, my mother s name. I should like to know my father, myself unknown to hint. Help me, Miss Cavanagh. I have the courage to meet Dane Conyers day after day and not betray my relation- ship to him. help me to some disguise. Proem e for me this situation, and I will bless you all my days."

CHAPTER XLIII.

IN THE STRONCHOLD OF HER ENEMIES.

Wh'at is it?" asked Beryl. "Look—ii the carriage yonder !" cried Mrs. Margie. "Dr you see that man with the red hair and beard? Look, quick ! You see him ?" "Yes, yes," said 13eryl, shrinking back; "I see him." "What a fate that I should have seen him to- day, and for the first time in seventeen years ?" her excitement. " When did he get back to Eng- land? Mrs. Margie, relaxing her hold of Beryl in I can hardly believe the evidence of my senses ! Mrs. Desomml, that man is Colonel Gray, your own father Beryl looked after the luxurious carriage as it spell-bound.

-The man whom Mrs. Margie had pointed out to ter as Colonel Gray and her own father was Lord liawkhurst.

Beryl did not know him as the earl, but sho recognised him as the gentleman she had seen at Hawk hurst House, 'Thai man my father she ejaculated. "Yes, miss; your own father. I know him by his red hair, his big features, and his red, bristling beard. He has grown older, but he has not greatly changed. Those fair men do not show age as dark men do. It don't seem possible, but your father has turned up at last. That is he ; I'll swear to him." She continued to gaze after the receding car- riage, as in a trance. But when it had vanished beyond her sight, she bethought herself, with a start, of her prisoner. Turning hastily, she found that Beryl had taken advantage of her pre-occupation, and was gone. Mrs. Margie began a wild search for her, but Beryl had utterly disappeared, and the lodging- house keeper was forced to continue her way to Wells-street alone, but with her marvellous story of adventure at her tongue's end. It had happened that an omnibus had drawn up at the kerbstone to admit a lady who had signalled it. Beryl, seeing her opportunity, had also entered it, and was some rods away before Mrs. Margie had discovered that she had vanished. Beryl returned to Bayawater without further adventure. She had scarcely removed her outer garments and seated herself by the fire to think over the fateful discovery of her father, when Miss Cavan- agh knocked at her door and entered the room. The landlady looked angry, suspicious and deter- mined. " Excuse my intrusion, Miss Star," she said, "but I came up to bring a note which a gentlemen left hero during your absence. He is your cousin, Kate says." Beryl s face flushed all over, but she took the note which Miss Cavanagh extended to her. It was sealed. " Tha gentleman called to see you, and seemed concerned because you were not in," continued Mt and left i iassCavatniat.e. "So he wrote that note, sealed Beryl tore open the envelope. The letter contained but few words, and they were as follows : " BERYL : I must see you on important business. Shall call again to morrow evening at half-past nine o'clock. DANE." Beryl crumpled the letter in her hand and thrust it into her pocket. "Of course your business is your business and not mine, Miss Star," said Miss Givanagli, "and I'm not wanting to pry into your affairs, but I must speak my mind, that's all. The gentleman was handsome enough, but there was that in his face I did not like, although he understam.s blarney well enough, I'll say that for him. I wish to ask if lie told Kate the truth. Is he your cousin ?' Beryl hesij;ated. She would not lie, and she could hardly confess so much of the truth without confessing the whule.

Miss Cavanagh, In ving approved Beryl's bold project of seeking a situ •tin in the very strong. ho d of the girl's eneitließ, proceeded with vigor and enthusiasm to assi,t in its accomplishment. That afternoon the warm-hearted Irish woman went out upon a shopping ex [fedi Linn, and procured at a furnishing-s• op for theatre-players the chief essentials for Beryl's necessary disgtuse. She also purchased, at a ladies' outfitting establishment, widow's caps, some neck ruffles, a w.dow'a bonnet and long yid, and a black bombazine gown. The bitter required certain alterations, which she assisted Beryl to make that evening. Upon the following morning, Beryl made her toilet with Miss Cavanagh's assistance. A wash, in use among actors and actresses, transformed her from the fairest of blondes into a brunette. Her eyebrows were skilfully darkened. Her hair was fortunately very short, and a black wig was fitted smoothly to her head and coiled' into a twist behind. , A pair of gold-bowed spectacles; a long, straight, bombazine gown, a close-fitting widow's cap; a high neck-ruffle and deep frills at her wrists, with judicious padding about the figure, completely transformed the fair, slim girl into a seemingly elderly gentlewoman. "No one who had known you as Miss Stw would know you now," said Miss Cavanagh, in admiration of her own ingenuity and skill. " You look like a curate's widow; you do, indeed. Bat what shall you call yourself? You Will peed a new name, you know, in your new character." "You must give me a name, Miss Cavanagh," said Beryl. "It must -be respectable. There's everything in a name, sure, though Shakespeare didn't think so. And the name should not be odd, so that is would attract attention. I have it. You shall be Mrs. Jane Park. That's simple and sounds honest. '

"hike the name. Jane was my mother's name," said Beryl, softly. The girl put on her widow's bonnet and veil, a large black shawl, black gloves, and took up an Astrachan muff and declared herself ready. There was nothing in her appearance to suggest disguise. She looked, in this dress, as in her usual garb, the true and perfect lady. "Come down to my drawing-room, Miss Starr," said Miss Cavanagh—" I mean Mrs. Park. Sure I must get used to the name, or I shall spoil it all. I'll put on my own bonnet, and we'll go directly to Hwkhurst House."

They descended the stairs to the drawing-room, but had scarcely entered the apartment when there came a loud and peremptory ring at the garden. bell. Beryl looked out of the window as Kate hurried out with the key of the garden-door in her hand. •

"I fear y.ou are going to have a call, Miss Cavanagh," said Beryl. "I can see three gentle- men outside—"

" It's the parish rates, perhaps," said Miss Cavanagh. "I may have to see them, worse luck, but they can't detain us long. Trust the parish officers for corning promptly." But they were not the parish officers to whom Kate gave admittance. Beryl drew back in the shadow of the curtain, recognising the visitors. They were Noble Desmond, Jr.D Sherwald and William Star of Starwood. Beryl retreated toward her landlady, with a countenance of dismay, explaming breathlessly who were the intruders. "They are coming in 1" cried Miss Cavanagh. "Was there ever such a stupid as Kate? Old Connaught has not the like of her. She's confessed you live here and that you're at home. BOVRIL.

We are promised a lawsuit of a singularly inter- esting character. Not very long since a well- known City mere haat left a co lossal fortune to cer- tain religious societies. The only exception made in the disposition of his property was that he gave to his three sons £lO,OOO each. They had to buy at a valuation the business which had enabled their father to accumulate his great estate, and they found the sum left them insufficient for the pur- pose. They have thus resolved to contest the will. , Terms have been offered to the religious societies, and some have decided to accept them. But those to which the larger amounts were bequeathed refuse, and a legal contest seems inevitable. The case will cause excitement in the religious world, inasmuch as the testator bequeathed the largest sum ever left to denominational purposes.

-- Litigation increases with the new judges. This is not a sign of more judicial wisdom, but of better times, the rule usually being "the more money, th more law, the more solicitors are threatened with removal from the roll." Divorce cases are failing in number. There are 32 libel and slander cases and 137 appeal cases. The total causes and appeals en- tered amount to over 2(00, or 264 more than there I were in the list for Hilary term last year.

Journalistic veterans are now seldom seen in Fleet-street, and in the day time least of all. The Dr. Johnsons of to-day go to the club to find them. There are over ninety journalists members of the National Liberal Club, including Germans, Aus- trians, Frenchmen, and Americans. The younger members of the literary profession are ot course always in evidence in Fleet-street, but they are un- known except to the yonog men. The street is a fine place, too, for meeting provincial scribes. There is only one place equal to It for "strange meetingq." and that is the outer lobby of the House of Com- mons. A walk in Fleet-street in the morning and a lounge in the central lobby in the afternoon and evening when the House is sitting will result in impact with the newest of new arrivals from every- where.

It is not improbable that there may be another batch of Carlyle letters to publish, some of them throwing further light on the career, the troubles, and the opinions ot the Sage ef Chelsea. His sister, who died some weeks ago in America, was in the habit of constantly receiving letters from him. I am informed that an examination of some of the letters has sufficed to indicate :..heir interest, and I either in America or in this co:intry, a selection from them will certainly be published.

-CAIJITAL" CHAT.

I understand that the Greek Government has ex- ' preessd its desire that a number of British officers may be allowed to go to Athens at an early date to take in hand the reorganisation and development of the Hellenic navy. The Government will pro- bably consent, especially as, failing any other ar- rangement the authorities at Athens would appar- ently be well satisfied to Lave the advice of officers connected with the Mediterranean squadron when in Greek waters.

It is a little disquieting to know that Sir Herbert Kitchener has retused to allow any newspaper cor- respondents to accompany the Soudan expedition even to Berber. I understand that he has caused it to be known that no correspondent will be allowed within fifty miles of the scene of any operations that may be undertaken,and that no exception will be made to this rule. Such a prohibition is likely rather to stimulate journalistic enterprise than to check it,and already I have heard of one American journalist who has left London for Cairo with the object of forcing his way to the, front in spite of the prohibition. Such an order as that of the Sir- tar's may have a certain authority when only Egyptian troops are concerned,but now that British soldiers have joined the expedition Sir Francis Grenfell should have some voice in the matter. An appeal will certainly be made to him, and it is ex- tremely unlikely, in view of the incidents of the 1 Tiza campaign,that the military authorities will re, 1 sit a strong representation on the subject.

The proposal for a railway to India has lately been seriously discussed in commercial and politi- cal circles. As an alternative, it is urged that pres- sure should be brought to bear upon the Peninsular and Oriental Company to shorten the passage to Bombay or Colombo by five or six days. It is pointed out that while Russia has for years past been steadily opening up and linking the whole of the interior of Asia with her European dominions, we, on the other hand, have been ooing nothing in , those southern and western parts of the continent I which naturally lie within our sphere of activity. The Great Siberian Railway will bring Japan within fourteen or fifteen days of London. as against twenty-eight days via Canada or thirty-one days via the Suez Canal. Nor is this all. The provision of fa-t steamers between the Pacific terminus of the new Siberian route and the Australian ports will effect a saving of ten clear days for passengers and mails travelling to Australia ; and it is therefore urged that if the Peninsular and Oriental Company fail to meet the exigencies of the time there should be no hesitation in proceeding with a railway to India, with provision for through traffic as far as Calcutta or Madras for Australia-bound passengers. Such an undertaking could not, it is contended, be opposed an political grounds.

--- It is amusing to read of the claim made by the wirepullera of the politicians in office that there bas been a diminution of crime under the present Government. The undiscovered crimes in and about London are well-known, and few family men in town can be ignorant of the nervousness the past has created among the fair sex. Rail journeys and shopping in the e.ening are now being discontinued unless paterfamilias can attend as a body-guard. The matter is much more serious than is commonly suppesed, and whether the police know of it or not I fancy a very little impair would convince them of its reality. There is almost as much dread of going out at night now as there was in Jack the Ripper's time.

It would be an awkward and instructive quid pro quo for Germany if China were to retaliate by an irruption of Chinese immigrants, with their cheap labour and their peculiar habits, into the Fatherland. Chinese immigration has raised serious problems and complications in more quarters of the. globe than one, and there is some consolation In perceiving that the latest victim to the overflow population of the Yellow race in Russia itself. Siberia is beginning to swarm with Chinamen, and that to an extent which is giving Russia serious anxiety. The matter has been brought before the St. Petersburgh Governratint,which has just called, in consequence, for a return of the number of Chin- ese who have entered Russian territory during the last twelve months. I have learned that the Chin- ese overflow is going on to an alarming extent-- that is, alarming to Russian officials. It has already as regards Australia, frightened some ot our Aus- tralian colonies; it has given rise to troubles in San Francisco, and Europe, which has hitherto escaped I may yet be a victina. They work for little, and there is more than one question which they would revolutionise if they once turned their attention to Europe with an energy similar to that with which Europe has turned its attention to the appropria- tion and exploitation of their country.

-- There seems to be an uneasy feeling in many quarters in regard to the position of the English missionaries in Uganda at the present time. There are several English ladies there and although they have now all been called in from their out stations to the capital, there is no guarantee that they are safe. Indeed the more recent letters would seem to show that the situation is fraught with peril to the whole party of missionaries. The letter from Archdeacon Walker pointing out the hostility of the great bulk of the people to Europeans has al- ready been referred to in the press. An equally alarming letter has been receivedfrom Dr. A R. Cook, who writes that quite 50 pr cent of the people in the ctipital and at least 90 to 95 per cent of the people in the country dislike and detest the missionaries and their new religion. "This is not a careless statement," he says, ' but the deliberate opinion of Mr. Pilkington, who has had quite ex- ceptional opportunities of judging. The last war was definitely stated by M'Wanga, in a letter to the King of Kok', to have for its object the overthrow of Christianity, both Roman Catholicism and Pro- testantistn, and driving all readers out of the country and re-establishing the old heathen cus- toms and laws." This presents Uganda in a new light, and further developments will be awaited with the keenest anxiety.

Miss Cavanagh's Irish face was quietly impassive. One would not deem her cognizant of Beryl's whereabouts. The gentlemen were forced to accept the situation. "Have you any idea when Miss Star will be in, madam V' asked Mr. Sherwald, addressing the landlady. "Sure and how should I?" she replied. "I didn't even know that she was out. "Be sure to tell her, madam, that we are her friends, and that it is of the utmost im- portance that we should see her. Stay, I will leave a note for her." He drew out his note-book and a pencil, and wrote the following words upon a loose scrap of paper: "BERYL, MY OWN \Vim : A friend of mine, Mr. Sherwald, discovered your place of refuge yester- day by following Conyers, whom he suspected of knowing it. Your uncle is with Mr. Sherwald and me as I write these hasty lines, Mr. Star repents his cruelty to you, and will take you home to Star- wood, provided that you persist in keeping away from me. My poor little wife, I want to take care of you, to cherish you, to reason away this vagary of yours that Conroy lives, There is no doubt that he is dead. He hesitated an instant upon the address, then wrote the name, "Miss Star." "Will you give that to her, madam, upon her return ?" he asked, placing the missive in Miss Cavanagh's hands. The gentleman saw the lady in black as she passed out to the cab, and all were positive in their belief that she was no other than Beryl ; but the vehicle rolled rapidly away in an opposite direction, and tliey had no opportunity to even address her. On arriving at Hawlthurst House, in Park-lane, they were shown into the housekeeper's room and into the presence of its owner. I'll answer for her, and you know that my. word is good as gold. If a curate's widow--a respectable, refined gentlewoman—would suit his lordship, Mrs. Park would suit him." "He does not want a nurse," said the house- keeper. "His valet nurses him, this morning his lordship flung a book at the valet's head for some clumsiness. NOTES AND NEWS.

The " 9tandard " has received a letter enclosing a cutting from the "Frankfurter General Anzeiger," which asserts that the piper of the Gordon Highlanders, who when shot through the ankles continued to plav,was not a Scotchman, but a German, named Julius Graumann, from Hesse-Darmstadt. This delightful paragraph Is too good to be lost. We give a translation :—" The ' lion- hearted; Gordon piper is not a son of the Scottish High- landers,but a German from Hesae-Darmstadt, who is ac- customed to answer to the prosaic name of Julius Gra*. mann, Like many other Germans, he has taken service in the British Army, thrown in his lot with the Gordon Highlanders, made the campaign as a piper, and true German that he is,done his duty on the field of battle.

The most serious news from India is undoubtedly CCM ined in the brief telegram which announces 159 cases. of plague and 128 deaths at Bombay during the last week-end . The figures are small compared with last year's. when in one of the blackest weeks the deaths numbered 1,853; hut it is poor consolation to reflect that the plague is keg had than it was last year. Since that time the authorities have acquired experience, and the natives have become familiarised with the necessity for the rigid isolation and secluoion of all cases, suspected or actual. A second visitation of the plague is rarely so serious as the first, but in this case the proportion of deaths shows that the tvpe of the disease is as virulent as ever, and the area of 'contagion is undoubtedly larger than it was. Last year the plague was practically con- fined to the three towns Bombay, Kurrachee, and Poona but since the Bombay outbreak the whole country-aide has been ravaged. A " Standard " correspondent writ- ing about a month ago .placed the total number of deaths from the plague in the Pi esidency outside Bom- bay at 24,090, and declared that the one bright spot was the immunity of the capital. The recrudescence of the plague in that place is the most serious calamity that could have befallen the west coast of ladia.

Sir Thomas Lipton has somewhat further developed the cheap restaurant scheme for London, which he is to set going with £lOO.OOO. The results of his recent cal- culations show him that the restaurants—which are to be self-supporting, but not profit-bearing—can supply a substantial erinner for the sum of 4d. or 44(.1. This modest price is contingent, of course,on the dining-halls being very big affairs—rach capable of seating, say, 2000, people • and Sir Thomas sensibly intends that they shall be distributed over the West as well ss the East-end —wherever, in fact, there is a sufficient industrial population.

Real oysters are expensive in Palls, and so, with the objoct ofPuitirg slender purses, artificial oys- ters on the half shell have been invt nted, which are sold at a franc a cirzn,and they are PO cleverly made and look so nice and fresh that,once lemon juice or vinegar has been added,they cannot be distil guished frcm the real article,especially when white wine is tsken in con- , neetirn therewith. Tbe only genuine thing about these oysters is the shell, the manufacturers buying second- hand shells at a small cost, and fastening the spurious oyster in place with a tasteless paste. The municipal laboratory has not yet proclaimed the ingredients of which these bogus oysters are composed, but has an flounced that they are of a harmful character.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE.

The velvet toque is certainly more in favour than anything else. It may be of pl:tin, shaded, embroidered or jewelled velvet, it cm have fur borderings, or its trimmings may consist of just a tuft of cock's plumes at the side, but thy crown is inevitably full and soft, and, the brim of folded material. Grey velvet with grey ostrich tips at the side, and twists of rose-coloured silk under the brim make a stylish toque. Shaded blue velvet, again, with purple velvet pansies, and loops of pale blue ribbon and cream lace, is another mixture which is considered decidedly up to date. The number of combs and pins and brooches with which a fashionable woman will adorn her hair nowdays, is quite astonishing, and too often the result is sometuing uncommonly akin to tawdriness, aot to say vulgarity.

Hats are all smaller, and not so excessively tilted, and the all-round drapery of Velvet is most popular, and sometimes accompanied by a narrow band of fur, or by lace, arranged round the crown, and showing above the drapery, or lying on the brim below the folds of velvet. Seine already wear the hat flat in front, and have rosettes or flUWers under the brim at the hack; but the side-trimmed hats are bare behind, and show rosettes only under the panache of plumes. Very piquant are the highwayman coats with treble capes for very little girls or boys, the diGrence in out being almost nil, but that worn by the girl is usually long, while the boy wears his barely to the knee. Blue, violet, and green cloth are all used for this type of coat, and in a pretty Lincoln-green, with beaver collar. The back is quite in coat style, with centre lapping below the waist as if left open, but there are no pleats or extensions beyond this lopped back, and the ful- ness is obtained entirely by gores. Made in beaver-backed cloth this requires no lining, merely facing the fronts with silk after fixing broad strips of canvas to hold the buttons and buttonholes. The flaps and cuffs are also interlined with canvas, and stitched round before being laid on the coat, and the edge of the double- breasted front is also finished with machine.. stitching.

For a tall, womanly girl of about 14 or 16 a full bodice is most suitable, and with groups of tucks and little frills of lace is really charming. The •elbow sleeves are also tucked, and finished with Pouffs, and there is a ribbon falling from the left shoulder, under which the dress fastens. The gored skirt is trimmed with vandyked frills of !ace. Cream-white cr6pGn or veiling, with yellow lace and either white or cream•coloured moir4 ribbon, is charming in this style. The mild weather has not brought forward the long, winter wrap, and short jackets, Russian blouses, and smart capes have been amply warm enough, and the large pelerine or Parisian collet have been useful for rinks and afternoon visits. It has been all too warm for sealskin, which has only been tolerable when driving, and quite im- possible for walking on the majority of days this winter. Rouse-dresses, pure and simple, thick " Musco- vite " cashmere is much fancied, and the styles in which it is made up are pretty enough. A gown of deep peacock blue, for instance, has its pouched bodice pleated into a small round yoke of ivory cloth, covere& closely with braiding in peacock blue and gold, a narrow band of blue velvet edging it. The sleeves have double cape epaulettes of cashmere • edged with velvet, and bands of braided cloth finish off the wrists. The belt is velvet, as is the plain

A veir.Y dressy costume e grey aaasibmere has the skirt edged with bands of moire-velours, and the bodice made with revers of ivory satin, covered with black lace appiique. and bordered with a narrow &trip ot the velours wbiela matches the cash- ratere in enlour. There is a pretty vest of tucked ivory mousseline de soie, and the belt is of velours, faith handsome steel ornaments back and front. Velvet miscues are seen on many of the thicker ,serge and cloth gowns, the revers or yoke ;matching. Buckles or brooches of similar form are some- -what replacing the insignificant safety-pin on evening gownst, and are seen at their best amongst draperies of lama or tulle. Embroideries in which 'rnitstion gems, cup sequins, and crystal heads are 'lf idly combined are used on the more elaborate of evening gown, and narrow strips are ved as braces. straps, and waistbelts an the ile toilettes of gauze and chiffon. Your loving JENNIE.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

„ Little Princess Victoria of Wales grows apace, and needless to say she is a tremendous pet at Sandriogham. Her small floral Highness is a very bright and lively child, and resembles her elder brother ” Kieg David " in disposition and tempe, ament. Prince" Bettie "is more sedate, but he is nevertheless, a winsome little fellow. At the coronation of the Queen of Holland, Queen Victoria, it is said, will be represented by the Duke and Duchess of Connaught, The Duchess of Albany too, as aunt of the young sovereign, has also been invited to at- tend the ceremony, and subsequent festivities. Queen Wilhelmina is a much prettier girl than any of her por- traits would lead one to expect. Her eyes are so blue, her cheeks so pink, and her smile so bright, that, apart from regularity of feature, she conveys a very agreeable impression. Her countenance shows signs of that deci- sion of character without which no woman is fit for a prominent position, but the sweetness of expression about the eyes very comfortably balances the firmness so legible about the mouth. The young Queen is an ex- cellent linguist, being able to speak five languages be- sides her own, English being among the number. The Dutch woman has strong domestic tastes and affections. When married she is devoted to her home, and is generally a good wife, and an ideal mother. The dowry is almost an unknown thing in Holland, except among the very wealthy classes. Its absence, however, does not prevent the girls from finding husbands,though ,it must be largely responsible for long engagements, I which are the rule. Thi se often last for three, four, or even five years, but a e very seldom broken off. The breaking off of an engagement involve considerable scandal,and the knowledge of this doubtless does a good deal to confirm the constancy of affianced couples. To this day, marriages by proxy are contracted in Holland, and are mostly practised by Duchmen, who having gone abroad, prefer to marry the girls they left behind them in this convenient manner, than to go to the expense, and probable inconvenience of returning to Holland for the purpose ot the ordinary marriage solem- nizatien. After the legal preliminaries are executed, a friend marries the bride by proxy, and she is therafter promptly shipped cff to her new home. Talking of marriage, the latest fashion in wedding rings, may be an item of interest to some of my readers. The all important circlet looks like an ordinary, rather narrow plain gold specimen, but on the inner side is a tiny round hole, and if the point of a pin be inserted therein, the apparently solid ring springs apart, into two linked circlets, en one of which is traced the initials of bride and bridegroom and the date of their marriage, while on the other is engraved some favourite motto. Lady Ottoline Dentinal, half-sister of the Duke of Portland, who is studying for the degree of M.A. at St. Andrew's University, is a great favourite with her brother's tenants in Caithness. Occasionally she gives a series of magio-lantern entertainments at all the little villages about Longwell House, and the cotters, whose festivities are few, much appreciate her efforts to give them pleasure. The Duke's deer forests, and grouse moors of Braemore and Langwell, comprise about 55,000 acres, and they usually afford excellent sports. Large bags of grouse are obtained, and both forests carry a heavy stock of red deer, which are remarkable for both size and quality. Apropos of forests,that of Windsor, which was made a Royal Forest by William the Conqueror, possesses not only the charm which its bistorical associations give it, but has,in itself, great natural beauty. From its height very extensive views may be had over a rich and beau- tiful country,and its splendid specimens of oak, beech, birch, and Scotch firs, and cther forest trees, its noble avenue of elms,and its fine sheets of ornamental waters, combine to make it a scene of great interest. Here, stands the King Oak, which tree is said to have been a favourite one of William the Conqueror, also the four trees known as the Queens'trees,which were selected re- spectively by Queen Anne, Queen Charlotte, Queen Ade- laide,and Queen Victoria. Elerne'soak,immortalised by Shakespeare, fell in 1863. Her Majesty has a cabinet made of the wood. .

There are numbers of women workers in our great cities, who much prefer one good sized room to live in,to two small stuffy ones, and a very ingenious, inexpensive set of furniture, devised to meet their requirements has lately been exhibited in London. The bed, six feet by four, has a French box mattress, and has a little wooden railing which runs round three sides of it. The rail is stained green, and a deep frill of cretonne is hung against it. This is a couch by day' and a bed by night. By day the bed clothes are covered with an ample cre- tonne cover, and the big rquare pillows are slipped into cretonne covers to match and sit up againit the rail, as cushions, A tell cabinet with doors above, and drawers beneath, is a disguised washstand. The door open, a Iflap turns down, and behold a set of green ware. On the inside of one door is a towel rail, and on the other a mirror. Holes, in the sides of this cabinet, permit the air to pass through. Then there is a little square table also stained green, which, open, is the handiest little toilet table in the world, while closed, it serves as a writ- ing table, or for tea. From Paris comes the news to the effect that a revival ' of the " chignon " will probably take place, in the spring. I remember, when I was quite small,l used in church to sit behind one of the belated adherents of this uatidy- uncleanly, grotesque mode. I called her the "bird's- nest-lady," and regarded her enormous excrescense with the fascination of horror. Like the " improver " scare however, the" chignon "scare may end in smoke. And most probable it will, for we are all too athletic nowa- days to find time and opportunity for such adorn- ment? .

, In the trimming of large hats, few things prove more satisfactory than long curling ostrich feathers. These plumes, now in such high favor, lend themselves very kindly to almost any sort of artistic arrangement, and they need but a clever touch here and there, to form a pertectly successful trimming to a low-crowned, wide- brimmed velvet hat. A capital method whereby to clean paint that has become dirty,is to provide a plate with some of the best whiting, and have ready some clean warm water, and a piece ef flannel, which dip into the water and squeeze nearly dry. Then take as much whiting as will adhere to it, epply it to the painted surface, when a little rub- bing will instantly remove any dirt or grease. After which wash the part well with clean water, rubbing it dry with a dry, soft cloth. Paint thus cleaned looks as well as when first laid on, without any Injury te the most delicate colours. It is far better than using soap, and does not require more than half the time and labour. A mode of procedure many of us are all too prone to indulge in at this season of the year, but one we should do well to carefully avoid is the use of a hot water bottle to the feet at night. It makes the skin tender and much more liable to be caught by the frost. Lambsweol bed socks must be worn instead, and before getting into bed the feet should be bathed in warm water, and be well frictioned for ten minutes with one of the rough Turk- ish gloves. Here is atapital way in which to use up scraps of cold meat. Butter half a dozen custard cups, (tea-cups with- out handles will answer the purpose)and fill them lightly with equal quantities of stale breadcrumbs and cold meat, chopped fine and seasoned with a dash each of salt and pepper. beat two eggs, add a cupful of milk, and divide among the cups. Set the cups in a pan of hot water,and bake in a moderate oven until firm in the centre. Turn out in a dish, and serve. MADGE."

DEATH 4JF MIL -ELIJAH HARRIS.

The inhabitants of Padgate and the surrounding district will hear with regret of the death of Mr. Elijah Harris, Daisy House.' Padgate, which took place at his residence between six alid seven o'clock last Friday morning. Although not altogether unexpected his end came lather suddenly and as a great blow to his many relatives and friends. Mr. Harris who has been comfined to his bed for about two months, has been troubled with hio heart and lungs for along time and gradual de- cay is attributed as the cause of his death, which is greatly lamented. Be has been medically attended by Dr. White of Warrington and Dr. Leach of Man- chester, who did everything possible to restore him to health. Deceased has been the head of the firm of Mecsrs. Thewlis and Griffiths of Mersey-street, Warriagton, for some years. He was at one time superiatendent of the Padgate Wesleyan Sanday school and whilst in good health was always a re- gular attendant at the Wesleyan Church. He was a member of the Padgate Parish Council at the commencement of that Council and afterwards re- tired. He will be much missed not only in the home but in the works, the church, and also the Parish where he has so long resided. He leaves a Widow and one daughter, to whom the deepest sympathy is extended.

A GREAT VICTIM TO BRONCHITIS.

A representative of the " Ashton-under-Lyne Repor- ter" made it his business not long age, to investigate the circumstances of a curious affair that had been rumoured about the town, and to ascertain the truth or otherwise of the statement. " I was told," says the re- porter, " that the subject of this remarkable report was Mr. Edwin Taylor, of 187, Old-street, Ashton, and to that address I wended my way, knocked at the door,and was fortunate in being received by Mr. Taylor himself. On stating my errand, he extended a hearty welcome to me, and expressed his willingness to impart all the in- formation I desired to seek, and even anxious that the circumstances should be universally known through the columns of this journal. "Mr. Taylor is a bacheler, about 40 years of age. De is a faetory operative by trade, having, up to about six months ago, worked in the cardroom at a local Owing, however, to being a sufferer from bronchitis,he had to relinquish that oecupation,as the hot atmosphere of the room,and the dusty particles which are given off in the preparation of the cotton, was more than his weakened lungs could bear. After leaving work, I fell,' said Mr. Taylor, ' into a very low,spiritless con- dition. My breathing was very difficult,and I had un- bearable pains across my chest. I could not sleep at nights, and altogether I fell into a very despondent state. I tried different remedies recommended to me by friends, but obteined no relief. At last a friend said he would bring me a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I told him it was no use, for I did not believe anything would do me good. Llowever,he brought me a box soon after, and after taking a few of the pills I found almost immediate relief. Continuing with the medicine, encouraged by this improvement, the second box effected an astonishing and gra- tifying change. I began to sleep better, and the

urch. tightness at my chest disappeared : in fact. I found sal- vation in those pills. I feel a different man altogether. I am organ blower at a local church,and that work is es- pecially trying to the chest and lungs, but I can do it without the least distress to my respirative organs. I continue to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, and I can honestly recommend them to others similarly affected. Such is the plain, unvarnished story of Mr. Taylor, who is only one of the thousands of living examples of the wonderful ctrative properties of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which have now attained a world-wide fame."

SOCIAL PROBLEMS. THE UNEARNED INCREMENT.

By JOSEPH RYDER, General Secretary of the Land Nationalisation Society.

ARTICLE XII.

If one man can command the land upon which othera must labour. be can appropriate the produce of their labour as the price of his permission to labour. The fundamental law of nature that her enjoyment by man shill be consequent upon his exertion is thus violated. The one receives without pro- ducing. the others produce without receiving. The one is unjustly enriched, the others are robbed. HENRY GEORGE ("Progress and Poverty.")

Mr. J. I'. Mason, a solicitor, at Christchurch. New Zealand, states that he investeel for a client in England £4OO in Chr stchurch properties thirty-one years HZO. Since then he has remitted in principal and interest £69,823, and he has over £lO,OOO worth ypt to realise.

"Land and Labour."

I could show that land for two miles round St. Paul's has increased during the last hundred and fifty years a thousand- fold in value, .

...- nOROLD ROGERS C' Work and Wages.") Of late years a great deal has been said and written about agricultural depression, and the consequent fall in agricultural rents. That there has been a great fall in rural land values of recent years is clearly attested by unquestionable statistics. But often what one landlord has lost another landlord has gained ; so that landlords in the aggregate have not suffered. On the contrary, it is certain that they have profited. For the unearned in- crement of the towns exceeds the unearned decrement of the villages. As a class, the landlords might console themselves with the resection to which the late Duke of Bedford gave utterance. He said, speaking of the re. ducedjrent rolls of his rural confreres,"And I,too,should no doubt be in a tight place, only that I luckily own a few lodging houses in Bloomsbury." Similarly, if we look upon the land as a whole, we should find that the increment of the one part more than balances the decrement of the other. The Duke of Devonshire's Irish estates must be set against the rents of Eastbourna and Barrow-in-Furness. The Duke of Norfolk's farms in Sussex bring him in less than formerly ; but neverthe- less his income becomes larger year by year ; for does he not own the greater part of that hive of industry, Sheffield. Tbe Lords Derby Can stand a lot of agricul- tural depiession while they own Bury and Bootl and even Lord Salisbury can afford to add farm to farm and field to field, as he is doing now in Hertfordshire, while he still draws a princely revenue from London rents. These are but a few of the instances where the landed claeses own town lands as well as country lands. But, it may be urged, they afford small comfort to the men , who only possess farm lands. Of course they do not. and lam not specially anxious to give them comfort. For it is certain that of the three partners in the agri- cultural industry they have suffered the least. And this I take from the mouth of Mr. Chaplin himself, the very High Priest ef Landlordism. At the great Agri- cultural Conference at St. James's Hall he said that the landlords had suffered a loss of 30 per cent ot their capital, and the farmers had suffered a lose of SO per I cent of their capital. And Al for the labourers, have they not been driven off the land by the hundred thous- and, and lest their employment, their whole capital ?

===== 0002601_18980114_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
IMIBARNED VALVES.

But, after all, to uaderstand the problem aright, it must be remembered that the real question is one, not of unearned increment or unearned decrement, but of unearned value. We have to consider not an much the rise or fall in the value of land,but in reality what is the true nature of that value itself. It is in the nature of all prices or values to rise or fall according to circum- stances. Hut all value may be divided into two great classes—land values and labour values. The former are unearnod by any individual or any class. They as(' the creation of the whole community. And whether they increase or decrease, they are rightfully common pro- perty. But the latter are quite different. They depend upon individual intelligence and industry, and preperty in them must be vested in their creators. Like land rent, they may increase or decrease. But an increase in an earned value does not thereby become an unearned in- crement. and if there be any right of private property in the 'first value, there is equally a right of property in its increase.

It is necessary to make this clear, because casuistical champions of the existing order are in- terested in confusing the issue by declaring that land values are net exceptional. Their method is like thia : (1) Land values and labour values are essentially the same. (2) Private property in labour values is perfectly equitable. (3) Therefore private property in I ind values is equally just. The validity of this reasoning is of course destroyed by the inaccuracy of the premise. Such inaccurate reasoning was employed in the de- fence of slavery; and for a long time it prevailed. It is only thirty-four years since a Liverpool audience re- fused to hear Henry Ward Beecher for his attack upon the Southern Slave States. But, as President Lincoln once said, " You can fool all the people some of the I time, and you can fool some of the people all the time, but you can't fool all the people all the time." Slave values were then accounted as legitimate private property. " What," said the sugar planter ; " after I have paid hard cash for the slaves and taken all risks, and paid overseers and whippers-in, and paid doctor's trifle,and erected huts for thetn,would you deprive me of my lawful property? You might as well a.k for my house,and for my horses, and for my bank balance. It would be just as fair." And yet, in case after case, Governments have emancipated slaves without the die- astrot2S consequences that were foretold. They have

A BACUP WOMAN'S TRIALS.

HEART DISEASE AND NERVOUS BRELEDOTIN.

NOW ENJOYS LIFE AND WORK.

Hearing that a woman residing in the valley of Res- sandals had undergone a most temarkable series of trials a reporter of the " Bacup Times" called at the small bet tidy dwelling of Mrs. Twiddy, of 20 Lodge-view, Leemill Bacup. Mrs. Twiddy heartily welcomed the reporter. " You could not have seen me better than as I am now," remarked Mrs. Twiddy, " for I am both washing and baking, and feel as strong as any woman, but four years ago I could not have done this work. I was weak and totally unfit for anything, and no one knows the struggle I had for existence. Mrs. Twiddy, in her own words, then related the story of her recovery. She said : " Four years ago, when living in Durham county, my condition was de- plorable. I suffered acutely from an affection of the heart. At times my condition was dreadful, for my heart seemed to stop beating. For years my complaint got no better, the slightest excitement would bring on palpitation of the heart, and other alarming symptoms. Then the climax came. I could go no longer, and was obliged to take my bed. A first-class doctor attended me, but I lost almost all my flesh, and had an all-gone sensation at the pit of the stomach which nothing would satisfy. My nervous system got exceedingly weak, and the wind on my stomach caused me anguish. These attacks left me frightfully exhausted, and with every fresh visitation they developed In severity,

On several occasions Mrs. Twiddy was watched as a dying person. She could not eat or drink lost her voice, and was almost bereft of her sense.. She then commenced to take Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and from that moment her appetite and voice gradually returned, and she grew to her present healthy state. " I feel better now," she says, " than I have done for years, and since March, 1893, when the cure was effected,l have increa- sed more than three stone in weight. I was given up by all the doctors, and have spent several pounds in medicines, but all to no purpose. ACCIDENT IN A WA.littEN6l-roN" BAKEEIOTJSE.

A WORKMAN CLAIMS £312.

Before His Honour Judge Wynne Foulkes at the War- I rington County Conrt last Thursday week, a case was commenced in which Stephen Ravenscroft Turner, I formerly in the employ of Messrs. Strange and Sons, bakers, of Latchford, claimed £312 I under the Employers Liability Act for damages alleged to have been sustained. Mr. J. Overend Evans, barrister, of Manchester, instructed by Messrs. Jenkins and Company, represented the plaintiff, and Mr. J. K. Bradbury, barrister of Manchester, in- structed by Mr. F. R. Roberts, appeared for the defend- ants. Considerable interest was taken in the case, the I court being crowded. A large number of bakers were present. In opening the ease, Mr. Evans said the action was brought under the Employers Liability Act to recover damages for personal injuries sustained by the plaintiff on the 11th October last. On that date whilst in the service of the defendants, the plaintiff lost his right hand, and that was said to have happened by reason of certain defects in the defendants machinery and owing to their negligence. The principal de- fects were the slippery condition of the floor near the kneading machine, and also the omission of a lid to cover the top of the machine. The defendant firm consisted of Mr. James Strangeddr.John and Mr. William Strange, and 'they carried on the business of bakers. The plaintiff was the foreman baker. About six years ago he was in the defendants' employ,and at that time he was using the same machine for some period of time, but then the slippery condition of the floor that he found later on did not exist. The plaintiff left the service of the defendants and was away for some years,until towards September of last year,the defendants sent for him again.lt was arranged that the plaintiff should be the foreman baker, and that he should receive £2 a week. The plaintiff started work on the 3rd of October. Soon after starting work the plaintiff found that the flior round the machine sass in a very slippery condition, and he complained about it to Mr. William Strange and Mr,John Strange,and they promised him that the state of things should be remedied. As a matter of fact it was not attended to by the time of the accident which took place on the 11th of the same month. The machine in question was known as the Universal kneading machine.and he understood the jury had seen it. Mr. Evans proceeded to explain how the flour was shot into the drum of the machine from the room above by means of a wooden bcx with a sleeve at- tached. Inside the drum there were certain revolving knives which turned in opposite direetions and performed the process of kneading. To this machine there was no lid or cover to guard the men from coming in contact with the knives. He contended that such a machine should not have been without a lid, and that was a dem-, in the machine. At the time of the accident the floor near the machine was in a slippery condition caused by an escape of liquor from the dough and the lubrications from the bearings and other parts of the machine. At eleven o'clock at night on the 11th of Oct. last the plaintiff was attending to the machine and was in the act of reaching a catch in the sleeve in order to let down some flour Into the machine below, when his foot slipped and he fell with his right hand into the revolving knives. The plaintiff found himself being pulled with tremendous force, and saw that the only way to save himseif from severe injury was to let his hand be cut off and then withdraw himself. This happened and the plaintiff's right hand was afterwards found in the dough. Besides the loss of his hand the plaintiff seemed to have ruptured himself with the struggles in trying to save himself. When the plaintiff was first caught in the machine he called out for some- one to come and assist him. No one, however, came to him until he had released himself from the machine,and blood was then spurting from his arm just as if it was from a syringe. Ultimately, the two assistants—Ban- croft, who was upstairs,and another Young man named Taylor,came upon the scene. When the latter saw what had happened he put his hand to his face and said he could not stand that, and ran out for assistance. A policeman, named Barnett, came in, and he having been instructed in the work of rendering first aid,very kindly and humanely bound the plaintiff's arm up and accom- panied him to the Infirmary. A second policeman came in later, and he took out the hand from the matlaine,and he would be able to say that nothing else was in the dough but the hand. One of Mr. Strange's sons had come on the scene before the plaintiff left for the Infir- mary, and he went to the plaintiff and asked him to say that the accident had happened whilst scraping the machine and whilst there was no dough in it, because he was afraid it would injuze his trade. He probably said this, feeling that the fact of a man's hand and blood having been mixed with dough might possibly have pro- duced a nasty sensation outside. The injury received by the plaintiff was such as to prevent him from earning anything like what he formerly earned. Mr. Strange, sen., went to see the plaintiff at the Infirmary and pro- mised to do certain things for him. The maximum cam-

found it. possible,as well as equitable, to separate slave values from other values. Is there a MAO in England who would now go back to the old system! And so it will be with land values. The mills of God grind slowly, But they grind exceeding small. Nations learn their lessons slowly, but gradually the sess'sm is being more surely are discarded, and better learnt than this, that the whole of the value of the land of a country is created by and should be owned by, the and Icnuda customs l onesold badar e systems adopted. whole community. By the term "unearned increment," then, we simply mean an increase in the value of land which is now appropriated by landlords, with the sanc- tion of laws made by landlords, with the sanction of laws made by landlord Parliaments in the past. TELE GROWTH OF RENT. The rate of interest tends to fall. But the rent of land taking it in the block, tends to rise. This is an import- ant difference that must always be borne in mind. Hallam tells us,in his "Europe during the Middle Ages," that arable land in England let in the thirteenth century for sixpence an acre, and meadow land for twice or thrice that sum. In the fourteenth century it was con- stantly obtained for ten years purchase. But in the fifteenth century, says Harold Rogers, it was valued at twenty years purchase. And by the middle of the seventeenth century the rent of land had increased twenty-fold since the Middle Ages. Arthur Young estimated the rental value of agricultural Eng- land at £16,000,000, or nearly IN. an acre. And, by- and-by, the wars, at the end of last century and up to the battle of Waterloo, sent up the value of land enorm• ously. It is an ill-wind that blows nobody any good. And so the incomes of the landlords were doubled and trebled by the very same :Anse that drenched a contin- ent in blood and brought sorrow to millions of homes. VIE INCOME TAX RETURNS.

If price depends upm supply and dernand,and you have a mu- lion pounds there in twenty- limited supply of land and a growing demand for it (by a population which is increasing in both numbers and wealth), it is inevitable that the rent of land must go SJ that the question to be solved is,should that in- crement enrich the idle classes, or should it benefit the whole people? There can be but one answer to such a question. For the present system which enriches the few with unearned wealth leaves many thousands of de- serving workers in a state of unearned poverty.

Nicotine-poisoning by veg—etables was, says the Paris correspondent of the " Chemist and Druggist," a scare started at Cannes last week, but there is very little in it. The theory was that persons who had eaten vegetables grown in a field in which the previous crop had bean tobacco-plants were attached by nicotine poisoning. There is, however, very little nicotine in the green tobacco plant ; and it is not a substance soluble in the soil. There is no doubt that vegetables grown in to- bacco-fields take a somewhat rank taste. The supposed cases of poisoning may be due to salts al copper or under- cooked vegetables. Mr. Eddison announces that he has made a new dis- covery of great importance, and certainly if it fulfills all that is claimed for it, not only will the great inventor have added to his laurels, but the iron trade •of the world willoss he says,be revolutionised. The discovery is of a metal which when mixed in certain proportions with smelted iron, renders cast iron RS tough as the same metal when wrought. Details of the discovery are not available for publication. The announcement has excited much interest.

Stephen Ravenscroft Turner, the plaintiff, said he had been a baker for 40 years. Some years ago he was in the employ of the defendants for the second time on the 3rd October last, and continued there until the 11th when the accident took place. He noticed that the floor was wet and slippery from what had fallen from the machine. The machine leaked. On Saturday after- noon, the 9th of October, he called the attention of one of the defendants to the state of the floor. On the night of the 11th October, witness told an assistant named Bancroft to go upstairs and send some flour down. Wit ness was going to put his hand to the sleeve when his foot slipped on some oily substance and his right hand went inside the machine. The revolving knives caught his hand and dragged him down. He shouted for help, but no one came tor some time. His hand was cut off, and he fell down near the machine. He was afterwards taken to the Infirmary where he remained five weeks. After the accident had taken place, Mr. William Strange told him to say that he was scrap- ing the machine when ths accident took place. This was not the case, and the accident was due to him slip- ping. Mr. James Strange visited him in the Infirmary and said he was very sorry the accident bad happened, and be would do all his power to belp him.—Cross-es- amined by Mr. Bradbury : There was no threat of any legal proceedings at the time witness was in the Infirm- ary. Mr. Strange did not give witness 103. a week whilst in the Infirmary, but he increased his (witness's) boy's wages from 7s. a week to 17s. Mr. Strange promised to push the boy on. It was after that when witness was seen by an official of the Bakers Union from London. Two officials from the Union saw him in the Infirmary. It was after their visit that he threatened to take legal proceedings. Witness did not tell a man named James Cotham when in the Infirmary that the accident took plao3 when he was scraping the machine. He told Cotham that Mr. Strange bad told him to say that. The officials of the union saw him after he had told Cotham that. It would be a very dangerous thing to do to clean the machine whilst in motion. He denied that he told a man named Grounds when in the Infirmary that he had been cleaning the machine, and dropped something into into it,and that whilst trying to get it out his hand got caught. He did not tell him anything like that. He I admitted having had a conversation with Grounds about Ithe accident,but he told him that he was in the act of reaching the catch to let the flour down when be slipped into the machine. He could not say why these witnesses should come and tell lies about him,unless they had been put up to it. Is was entirely untrue that he told a visitor to the Infirmary named Drewett that it was a pure accident. " I was cleaning the machine with a scraper when I got my hand caught." He did not suggest that that witness had come there knowing it to be false at the instigation of someone else. He remem- bered speaking to a young man named Kinder when in the Infirmary. Be told him that he Blipped into the machine. He did not say that he was cleaning the machine whilst in motion, and that he had a scraper in his hand. The four witnesses mentioned were telling what was false. He did not know them before he went into the Infirmary,and he had no reason to suppose that they had a grudge against him. He was certain that Mr. 1 William Strange came to him just before he went into the Infirmary and told him to say that he was scraping the machine, because it might hurt their trade. There were several others in the bakehouse at the time, but they would not be able to hear what was said, as it was whispered into his ear. It was un- true to say that Mr. William Strange never entered the bakehouse at all, and that he went straight away for a conveyance. When Mr. Strange visited him witness told him what Mr. William Strange had told him to say. Mr. Strange asked him how it happened, and witness told him that he slipped when reaching the catch te let the flour down. Mr. Stranee was known as an honourable tradesman in the town. He promised witness all kinds of things. Witness did not

The floor was swept every day after the work had been finished. If these was anything wrong with the floor it was his duty te tell one sf the assistants to put it right. The floor was as wet and greasy as ever soon after it had been swept up. There was no tin to catch any dripping from the machine. A tin was on the floor but it did not catch the drippings. He complained about the state of the floor to Mr. William Strange who promised to see to the matter. Witne6l worked the machine five years ago for a few weeks. The floor was all right then. THE EXAAINER.

JANUARY 14th, 1898.

Tun Plymouth election, which The Plymouth has resulted in the return of the Election.

Liberal candidate, very clearly enforces two or three .important lessons. In the first place it adds one more to the indications, which have been pretty numerous of late, that the "flowing tide" is now with the Liberal party. It is true, of course, that the election does not in- increase the number of Liberals in the Reuse of Commons as the member whose death caused the vacancy was himself a Liberal, but at the same time in moral weight it is really equal to a clear gain of two votes on a division. Plymouth is one of the comparatively few double-seated constitu- encies which now remain in this country, and at the General Election in 1895 the successful Liberal was a minority Member. A Tory, Sir EDWARD CLARKE, was at the head of the poll, an d another Tory only missed the second place by twenty-seven votes. In this bye-election, therefore, what the Liberals had to contend against was a Tory majority cast so recently RS 1895. At the three previous elections, that is to say, in 1885, in 1886, and in 1892, the Toriea carried both seats. Under these circumstances, therefore, the victory of Wednesday may be fairly claimed as equal in moral weight to the gain of a seat. It was only won by turning a minority of votes into a majority. The poll is an exception- ally large one, in fact the largest that has ever been cast in Plymouth, and that too is a most satisfactory feature from the Liberal point of view as it shows that the electors were fully alive to the issues at stake and that the election was not won through the apathy or indifference of the people. Nor can it be said that the result has been influenced by the local or national influence of the Liberal candidate. Mr. MENDL is evidently a young gentleman of spirit and ability but he does not appear to have any special local claims upon the Plymouth people, whilt he has been de- nounced by his opponents as a "German Jew," ' though born and reared and educated in England. The party which a:most deified Disraeli and which at the present time does not scorn to accept the assistance of the Rothschilds is still quite prepared, when it suits its convenience, to asperse an oppon- ent on account of his Jewish blood and religion, and there can be little douht that the calumnies which were propagated from _ Tory platforms would have some effect amongst the more ignorant and prejudiced portion of the electorate. Yet, in spite of all these adverse influences, the Liberals have won by a majority which is fairly substantial for a town like Plymouth which is famous for its close fights. A second lesson that may be derived from the contest is the fact that even in such a centre of military and naval activity as Plymouth the militarism of the present Government is not approved. The people of Plymouth, like the people of other parts of the country who have recently had an oppertunity of expressing their opinions through the ballot boxes, are not proud of a Government which, through truckling to European despots, has shamefully betrayed the hopes of the persecuted and struggling Christians of the Turkish Empire, whilst it has rushed with- out rhyme or reason,into a disastrous and unneces- sary war on the Indian frontier, A particularly gratifying matter to temperance people is the prominent part which local veto and temperance reform generally has played in this contest. The Tory eleetioneerers, from Sir EDWARD CLARE downward, have loudly and persistently declared that local veto is dead, but they have found to their cost that it is still very much alive. Mr. Gunn., the Tory candidate, in the earlier days of the contest declared that he was opposed to temperance reform, and in return for this he secured the vigorous support of the licensed victualler*. But while he gained

on one hand he lost on the other. There are in Plymouth, Conservatives and Liberal Unionists, as well as Liberals, who honestly believe that temperance reform is one of the most important political questions of the day, and some of these did not hesitate to endorse their convictions by signing a Liberal nomination paper, and no doubt voting for the Liberal candidate as well. This election is, in its way, therefore, a very suggestive commentary upon the proposal which has been made, even by some Liberal politicians, that local veto should be dropped from the programme. It confirms the opinion that has been found by many close observers, that there:is a growing feeling in the country in favour of a drastic reform of the liquor laws. Local veto, or local option, may not be the best of all possible methods of embodying this demand for reform, but. at present local veto holds the field and will continue to hold it until some other proposal is formulated which has better claims to the attention of temperance formers.]

Mountain-Climb- THERE must be a strange and irresistable fascination about ing. mountain-climbing, or we should never hear of the performance of such perilous feats as the ascent of Aconcagua which elevated pile now takes its place as" the highest moun- tain ever climbed." Mr. E. A. FITZGERALD has jest told,in the form of an interview with a repre- sentative of the "Daily Chronicle," the story of his gallant attempt to reach the highest peak of Aconcagua, the loftiest mountain in the South American Andes. Mr. FITZGERALD was not him- self successful in reaching the goal which was aimed at by the plucky little party of Englishmen and Swiss mountaineers of which he was the leader, as he was completely prostrated after scaling all but a thousand of the 23,000 feet. One of his companions and a Swiss guide were, however, more successful and after persistent attempts found themselves on the coveted summit. That mountain-climbing is not an exercise for invalids or for the physically weak goes without saying, and,as a matter of fact, it is evident that comparatively few men could stand the strain of living at high altitudes. Mr. FITZGERALD spent fourteen days and nights at an altitude of 19,000 in intense cold and with a gale blowing, and this is how he described his existence at that time. "You pant like a dying consump- tive ; then the dust, which smothers everything, gets into your throat and chokes you; you cough exhaustingly and pant worse than ever. Every effort, however slight, entails a fresh effort of will, and your only desire in the world is to give up the whole thing and get down. When I was not climbing I just sat and wished I was dead. I was constantly sick." The monotony of the situation is occasionally relieved by rather start- ling adventures. On one occasion, for instance, Mr. FITZGERALD was riding a horse amongst the snow when the animal suddenly disappeared be Death him and be was left standing with his feet on either side of the hole into which the horse had fallen.

The old folks of Warrington have had their "treat," and it is a strange fact that there is a de- crease of no fewer than 350 on last year's num- bers. We cannot conclude from this that such a large percentage have gone over to the majority, and must perforce accept the explanation of one of the officials that improved trade is the cause of such surprising abstention. We suppose such a conclu- sion is arrived at by the argument that better trade means better food at home, and therefore less necessity for the old folks to visit Parr Hall in search of a "square meal." The two large gatherings of the old and hungry have many amusing aspects, and perhaps the view of the diners of male persua- sion is the funniest. Students of physiognomy found Parr Hall a happy hunting ground on Thurs- day. There were lean faces, fat faces, clean faces, grimy faces, shaven faces, stubbly faces, with jaws moving in laughable unison. Many and varied,too, were the styles of eating. Each guest appeared to have been trained in a different school of etiquette. One could almost detect a man's trade from his table manners. There were some who conveyed the eatables to their mouths with an action sugges- tive of the trowel,and otherkstabbed their beef with an alarming ferocity. It was quite a transformation scene at tho close when signs of contentment were to be observed stealing across the countenances of the Old Folk's:Committee's venerable guests, and a slowing down of movements which were at the commencement quite energetic.
