ALLEGED FORGERY BY A FORMER

RUNCORN SCHOOLMASTER.

At the Stockport Borough Police Court on Saturday, Thomas Cockshott aged 27 years, formerly schoolmaster at Dutton Workhouse, Runcorn, was charged with steal- ing £8 by means of a forged telegraph money order, on the 27th November last.—Mr. Dobson,who appeared for the prosecution, said the prisoner was only taken into custody the previous day, and as they had to get evidence from Sheffield, he proposed to ask for a remand for a week.— Inspector Quigley stated that on the 27th November the prisoner called at the Stockport Post Office, and asked if there were any letters for a Mr. Samuel Meggitt. One was handed to him, and afterwards the prisoner, it was alleged, sent a telegram to Mr. Price at Sheffield (where Mr. Meggitt also resides), and £8 wag remitted to him by means of a postal telegraph money erder.The prisoner was that morning placed amongst six other persons and identified by the clerk at the Post Office who paid him the money as the man who signed the name ot Samuel Meggitt on the telegraph money order. Witness received the prisoner in custody from the Liverpool police, and charged him wig' stealing the £B, but he made no reply.—Tne bench remanded the prisoner for a week.

===== 0002601_18980114_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
BETHESDA. LIT-ERA RIC GUILD, RUNCO RN.

DEBATE ON LOCAL OPTION. The above society resumed its first session on Wednes- day evening, by a discussion of the subject, " Local Option, is it the best method of dealing with the Liquor question "? There was a very good attendance of mem- bers, and a few visitors from other denominations were present. Mr. James Bowyer led off the discussion with an excel- lent ancLinstructive paper,in which he upheld the neces- sity of a direct popular veto on the basis of a two-thirds majority of the inhabitants of any given district. Be claimed that at the present moment 2,000 parishes were entirely free from the baneful influence of the public- ly:ins° simply because certain landlords had decided that such houses should not be built upon their estates. Mr. Bowyer claimed that the same discretionary power should be vested in the pesple of any neighbourhoed. Mr. Jones, the free public librarian, contributed a vigorous speech, in which he deprecated the action of the Right Hon. Herbert Gladstone,in trying to make the liquor question a non-party ques,don. Mr. Jones was strongly in favour of direct populor control. Mrs. Baker, of Mcughland-lane, spoke in warm appre- ciation of total abolition by the State, of the manufac- ture and sale of intoxicating liquors. Mr.W.Handley continued the discussion by taking up the idea that neither local veto nor prohibition were in themselves ideal schemes, emphasising the necessity of education alongside of every effort of the State. Miss Clayden also spoke, and earnestly pleaded for a wise consideration of the care of the minority. She deprecated the intemperate zeal of temperance reformers. Mr. Anderson, in the course of a short speech, said he sympathised with the remarks of the last speaker. Mr. Eric Speakman and Mrs. Osborne also spoke. The Rev. J. Osborne, 8.A., pastor of the church, brought the discussion to a close by a speech in which he advocated the State regulation of the manufacture and sale of spirits. The State by itself would never finally abolish the evils of intemperance. The only possible guarantee lay in the co-operation of the State with the influence of the church, sr, in other words, tke final guarantee for a sober England lay in the education of the moral sense of the people. An interesting evening was brought to a close by a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. Bowyer for his excellent paper. •

ST. J OHN'S PPESBYTE RIAN CHU RC H, RUNCORN.

MARRIED LADIES' TEA.

In connection with the above place of worship the congregation are making strenuous efforts to get funds necessary to build the long contemplated Church. The different sections composing the congregation, namely, the married men, married women, and single men, and the children have all devised some means of augment- ing the fund which has been established for the purpose named. Tea parties and entertainments appear to be in the minds of all as the single men and married men and children have given theirs, and on Wednesday night the married ladies had theirs, so that it now only remains ,for the single ladies to take their turn. The event on Wednesday was held in the school-room, Victoria-road, there being an extraordinary attendance several having to be refused admission. A splendid tea was provided, the following, ladies presiding at the tables :—Mesdatnes J. Hewitt, F. Hewitt, Gatley, Dob- son, Warburton, Timperley, Burn, Jackson, Hough, Williamson, Baxter, Thomas, and Jones. After the room had been arranged the concert was held. The Chair woman was Mrs. Richardson, who, in the course of an address, said she was astonished, but nevertheless de- lighted with the success that had so far I attended the efforts of the married ladies ef St. John's. The ladies who were to entertain the company for the evening dressed in crinkled piper hats and all wearing rosettes, then marched on the platform and sang the chorus" Beautiful Gifts of God." Mesdames Burn Warburton, and Timperley amused the audience by their selection on guitars. Miss Emily Hughes of 1 London, daughter of the late Mr. H. Hughes, who for many years was the manager of the Bridgewater Docks, and who afterwards removed to a similar position in London, gave a reading. Miss Hughes has been edu- cated in Germany and is well known in this country in 1 1 literary circles. She has teen well-trained in the art of 1 elocution, and her appearance in her native town was ' loudly applauded. She kept her listeners in rapt atten- tion during the whole of her reading. Mrs. Vfilliamson sang a solo, the chorus being taken up by the ladies. For her sone " A wreath of Roses," Mrs. Temperley re- ceived a well merited encore. She replied with " Coming through the Eve." Mesdames Dobson and Kirkham were attired the former as a Scotch lass and the latter as a Scotch lad, and sang a duet. The chorus " Sleigh- ing song" by the ladies was well received. whilst the solo and chorus by Mrs. Hough and the ladies, entitied "Laugh and grow fat "caused roars of laughter. The reading entitled " My mother never did it so," by Mrs. Jackson was loudly applauded. Some lines composed Iby two of the married ladies, and sung by the whole troupe caused immense fun. The enjoyable entertain- ment was concluded by the ladies singing the chorus " Auld Lang Syne" and " Men of Harlech." Votes of thanks were proposed, to whiub the chairwoman re- sponded.

PRESENTATION TO THE LATE ORGANIST OF HOLY TRINITY, RUNCORN. INTERESTING GAT SEEING.

At the Holy Trinity Schools, on Friday evening last, a very interesting and pleasant event took place.namely, the presentation ef a gentleman's teilet case and a cigarette case to Mr. A. Savage, late organist at the Holy Trinity Church. The choir sat down to a hot-pot supper, provided by Mr. and Mrs. J. Horsefield,Mr. and Mrs. Burrows, Mrs. Johnson, and Mr. Hankinson, after which the interesting part of the proceedings com- menced. Mr. W. E. Lancaster, choirmaster, said he had a very pleasing task to perform in presenting their late organ- ist, Mr. A. Savage, with a small token of their esteem and affeetion,consisting of a gentleman's toilet case and a cigarette case. Mr. Lancaster referred to the capabi- lities of their late organist, and said he considered he was an organist of such merit that he would be able to take a service in any church in the country. As well as being a good organist and musician he was a first-class organiser, and his resignation would certainly be a very great loss. (Applause.) Mr. William Hankinson, the people's warden, referred to the time when Mr. Savage was organist at the Sun- day school, under his superintendence. The progress he then made was most gratifying. He considered that although Mr. Savage had seen fit to resign his position as organist, it was a source of great pleasure to hint to know that he was continuing his connoci.,ion with the Sunday school as a teacher. (Applause.) Mr. C. A. Timmins, the Vicar's warien, said he was sure that Mr. Savage would always remember with pleasure the happy and profitable time spent as organist of Holy Trinity Church. Anyone who had had experi- ence of voluntary services knew how difficult it was to maintain regularity and discipline. Although Mr. Savage had thought fit to resign from taking actual ser- vice,thry hoped he would allow himself to be a reserve, and in that capacity they all wished him health, happi- ness,and prosperity, in his future life. (Applau,a.) Mr. Lancaster then handed the presents to Mr. Savage, and the company rose and sang heartily "For he's a jolly good fellow." M. Savage, who was deeply moved, thanked them for their gifts and said he would ever cherish the good feel- ing of those with whom he had worked so long, and in so friendly a way. (Applause.) A hearty Vote of thanks was accerded to those who had provided supper. The remainder of the evening was spent in harmony. Mr. A. C. Timmins who had been appointed organist at Holy Trinity Church, commenced his duties on the 2nd inst. He was for several years a chorister in Holy Trinity Church, and at the age of 13 years won the Crewe Scholarship at Liverpool for sight singing. For the last four years he has acted as deputy organist at St. Michael's Church, under Canon Wood.

kilt WALTER SCOTT.

LECTURE AT RUNCORN.

On Tuesday evening in the Brunner Guild Hall, Run- corn,a lecture was given on "Sir Walter Scott " by Mr. J. White,of Runcorn, under the auspices of the Runcoin Pickwick Literary Society. There was a very large-at- tendance, presided over by Mr. T. Saunders, president. In the course of his opening remarks, the Chairman said this was the Pickwick Society's fleet public venture in providing for the people of Runcorn a literary treat. The Pickwick Society was formed with the intention of its becoming a factor in the upraising of the literary tone of the town. The original founders conceived the idea that there was yeteroem amidst the many institu- tions of the kind already established, for another similar venture,but with a broader basis to work upon, both in respect to subjects, and the manner of conducting the meetings. They know no religious or political creed, and he (the chairman) would like to emphasize this point,it it might appear to some, looking at the manner of its inception that it sprang from some particular de- nomination in the town. If there was one thing whish hid added to the success of their society so far more than another it was the eothusiastn which had been dis- played by its members. They were not going to be one of those institutions,which, as was said a short time ago by an influential gentleman " flourished for a short time and then died away." No, on the contrary, as they felt week by week at their meetings the benefit which had accrued to them through mutually studying the subjects under discuenon, they felt convinced that in their own minds they dared not let an institution like that die away. That being the case they offered a cor- dial invitation to any persons who might he inclined to join their ranks as a literary society. (Loud applause.) Referring to the lecturer, the chairman said he was sure it was unnecessary for him to introduce Mr. White to the audience as he was well-known to Runcern people, and he was certain that his writings had been widely read by the people of his town. Mr. White, who was received with applause. then delivered his lecture on " Sir Walter Scott." Hassid Sir Walter Scott, like Dr. Johnson was particularly for- tunate in his biographer. Perhaps there was no flora in the whole history of English literature that stood out So prominently as the great lexicographer whom the fussy, consequential, but tender-hearted and devote 1 Bozzy "—as James Boswell was affec,ionately had immortalised. This did not insinuate that Dr. John- sen owed his popularity exclusively to the pages of Boswell. Johnson's own distinction as a great man of letters was sufficient in itself to secure for him a fore- most place in the gallery of English worthies. fait whether Posterity would have had the De. Johnson whom it n es, knew and loved if his life had been written by any other man than Boswell was a ques•ion which a consensus of literary opinion b settled beyond the shadow of a doubt. But the life of Sir Walter Scott, by his son-in-law and executor John I Gibson Lockhart, dlfered essentially from the life of Johnson by Boswell. The one was a faultless—or nearly faultless—work of art conceived and executed with the consummate skill of a practised writer, whose close personal intimacy with the subject of his book, enabled him to present a picture of the great man whose life he undertook to record the truthfulness and absolute veracity of which no dispassionate leader would venture to question. They would fail absolutely to understand the strong feudal bias which formed such a str.king fea- ture of Scott's character if they omitted to take into cos- sideration the ancestry of Sir Walter, whe was a direct descendant—six generations removed—of the Sir Walter Scott commemorated in the Lay of the Last Minstrel, as A eld Wat of Harden. There s the following romantic incident of William, on of Auld Wat.lt is said that dur- ing a raid of the Scotts clan,nn the lands of a neighbour- ing chieftain,Sir Gideon:Murray,Auld Wat's son,Willlam, had the misfortune to be captured by his incensed ad- versary. Instead of hanging him upon 1:113r eeriest tree, as would have been done by some jubilant captors, he gave him the choice of being banged privately, or marrying the ugliest of his (Sir Gideon Murray's) three ugly daughters, a lady who went by womenhed cuol ltrlayt .e gtcarriedolMeg, etandpo ruir. twh he ewu ap sh o ni o furthers,ful oe rni otapim ugliness aa t oid tMahhi neaai tkvo nteevg- IM7i:ahciimeet otfthe counties. prize W soner,was a remarkably hands m, man, some notion of the awkward predicament in which ha was placed by, twheislloiaffner soefoht,islickaepatogre,nsmibaleynbla!nin, fperr er ef edr.r edA earl svk r liars hopes of future happiness even with a wife whose chief feature was her grest ugliness, rather than cut short his career by the undesirable means of a rope. Nr did the courageous man live to regret his choice, tor not only did" Meikle-mouthed Meg," make him an exem- plary wife, but she became famous as a fine pickier of the beef which her husband stole from the herds of his foes. Scott himself was born in Edinboro' on the 15th August 1771. He evidently inherited his genius from his mother, who is said to have been a very remarkable woman, fond beyond measure of reciting the folk lore of her native land, of which her distinguished son was to give such an expression in his writings. Tri3 is yet another instance of the mother's power over the child. It is the highest destiny of women to become the heaven-sent instrumentality of watching over and ten- derly but surely fashioning the mental and moral charac- ter of the children whose futures whatever it may b.)- come is divinely placed in their hands.

Greet statesmen sway the nation, Kings mould a people's fate, But the unseen hand of velvet, The giants regulate, In that wee blanket curled The nations doom hangs on the babe, And the hand that racks the cradle, Is the hand that rules the world, Scott was throughout life a hard, practical, sagacious man of the world, who took to writing poetry and novels because it paid him to do so, and because, no doubt, in the exercise of his superlative genius, he combined pleasure with profit. When only 18 months old Scott had a fever, which re. suited in a life long lameness. This physical infirmity was perhaps the very basis of Scott's greatness. Com- bined with general ill-health,which, however,he speedily out-grew, it tended to restrict, for some years, his great love for out-door sports. But this as the sequel showed, proved the best thing that could have happened to him. He read every book that came in his way, good, bad, or iadifferent, storing his mind with a Ptlultitudinous mass of knowledge which in a few years was to be put to such splendid use by him. Scott's early years were nut re- markable for any display of precocity. He was reputed to be rather dull as a scholar, his chief forte lying in a wonderful capacity for telling stories for the edification of his school fellows. His father had intended him to follow the same profession as hirnself,that of a writer to the Signet, and with this aim in view he was entered as a student of law at the Edinboro' University, the cradle of many famous men in Scottish history. Scott pursued his legal studies with ardour and was eventually called to the bar. Every spare moment that he could steal from his legal studies was spent in roaming about the country, exploring every nook and corner hallowed by the dim traditions of the past, and collecting material for his Minstrelsy of the Scottish Border, a work which formed a fitting prelude to his own masterly creation. No man made more friends than Scott, or Anew so well how to keep them. Gentle and forbearing in disposition, with not the slightest trace of snobbishness or egotism in his whole nature, he wound himself :irresistably into the very heart of his friends. Sir Walter Scott like most men had a romantic love story, bu. owing to the opposition of the parents of the young lady, it never matured, and we find him within a year marrying the daughter of an exiled French royalist, by name Miss Mary Carpenter. e can find no hint in all Scott's writings to lead us to suppose that he was other than extremely happy in his marital relations. About this time began the unfortunate connection between Scott and the Ballantynea, cc hich ultimately involved him in financial ruin. Ton crisis which eventuated in the downfal of Scott from the exalted pedestal which he had so Jong and honourably occupied gave to the world one more proof of how a great man, in the plenitude of power and popularity, can calmly consent ti sacrifice his very life—as Scott undoubtedly did—to repair the wrong he had done, and to leave to posterity a fame all the brighter for the grand heroic strugglo which he had waged to redeem his name from the faintest suspicion of dishoeour. In 1805 Scott's great masterpiece " The Lay of the Last Minstrel," was published. Its 5,/,7,CeiS was instantaneous. Never before had poetry stirred men's hearts and fired their enthusiasm as this did. BY one wave of his magic wand Scott had eclipsed all rivals. The Lake School of poetry had not yet asserted its pre-emineace as the philosophic expression of "thoughts that often lie too deep for tears "—to quote Wordsworth. Byron and Shelly had not yet burst upon the world. Keats was yet a school boy. Crabbe and Thomas Campbell, Rogers and Tom Moore, alone held the .field. In the poetry of none of these men was there anything approaching to the poetry of Scott, whose creatiens bore the impress of living reality, combined with a rugged power and truthfulness of description which appealed irresistably to all. Scott's poetry comprising the Lay, Marmion, and the Lady of the Lake, did more to excite an inter- est in poetical literature proper than any other poetry published since the days of Dryden and Pope. He (the lecturer) excluded Burns, because his poetry occupied a distinct place of its own. Scott's poetry not only es- tablished a new school—that of narrative poetry—in which Byron was so soon to excel, but it had what no other poetry could claim, a distinctly commercial value. The land of Scott, as those parts of Scotland which the poet localised in his poetry are called, in- creased in value from the incursion of strangers, that immediately resulted from the publication of the poems. Scott himself also derived great pcuniary aid from the sale of his poems, and eventually Abbotsford was taken and enlarged, the total cost amounting to upwards of £29,000. About this time Scott began to be harassed for money by the Ballantynes, who had experienced a reverse of fortune, the result a 3 that Scott was forced to resort to some fresh expedient to relieve the exces- sive strain on his own resources and to satisfy the de- mands of his importuning partners. He well knew that he was being quickly out-distanced by the dazzling bril- liancy of Byron, whose poetry was now the rage of the hour. Nothing daunted Sir Walter Scott set about the completion ef a navel whish he had commenced same years previoasly, and thrown carelessly as not worth

Guy Mannering, perhaps the mew', fascinating of all his ro- mances, was completed in six weeks, the Bride of Lammermoor in a fortnighe Sir Walter Scott in his novels commits no offence either against morality,or the deeper, external virtues of existence. He was far too pure himself to pander to anything base or unworthy. His romances not only excite our interest,hut picture for us all that is best and purest in the outward and visible , world of God's universe. Hie vivid portraiture 3 of his historical characters are impregnated with the very breath of life. We realise at once that they are not the mere creations of a fertile fancy, but living men and women speaking Find acting like rational and sentient human beings. In nothing did he excel so much as in the delineation of some wild border out-law, characters ' demanding the colossal strength and vividness of percep- tion which were Scott's in a pre-eminent degree, rather than the delicate touch and analytic skill (that were not to his hand) of a later great novelist. Who that has read " Ivanhoe " can fail to realise and understand the knightly chivalry of the Black Princs or the maiden purity of Rebecca the Jewish maiden. SIR J. T. BRUNNER AND HIS GIFTS TO I NEW BOOKS, MAGAZINES, &c. RUNCORN. I --_

A meeting of the Runcorn Technical Instruction Com- mittee was held (-le Monday, in the Council Chamber, at the Town Hall, Mr. John Wilson presiding. The Secretery (Mr. E. Marshall) submitted the follow- ing letter which he had received from Sir J.T. Brunner : —"Druids Cros.,W svertree,Liverpool,7th January,lB93. Dear Mr.Marsliall,—l am h iartily obliged to your Coun- cil tor the handgome compliment paid to roe by the pas- sing of the resolution of thanks at their meeting yester- day. With r.,gard, however, to the authority given by the resolution to the Technical Instruction Committee to take steps towards the prt seating to me of a piece of plate, 1 13g with your permission to recall the fact that my recent gift was a token of my admiration of the pub- lic spirit shown by the people of Runcorn,and especially by Mr. S.Taylor,your chairman. It appears to me per- fect:y clear under these circumstances that 1 am entirely precluded from accepting any gift in return. Hewever, tell your Council that I record the unanimous passing of the resolution with the greatest possible pleasure, as one amongst many of the gracious acts on their part tending to cera eat our long-enda.ing mutual friendship. —Yours faithfully, JOHN T. BRUNNER. E. Marshall, Esq." The Committee felt bound to consider the wishes of I Sir John, but were of opinion that they should recognise his generosity in some form. I CASSELL AND Co.—Though Christmas is past, it has not been so long gone as to make less welcome the facsimile of Dickens' manuscript of" A Christ- mas Carol," which is issued in conjunction with Cassell's Magazine. Only a few copies of this liter- ary curiosity have so far been sold to the public,and then at a high price, so that the opportunity of procuring one for a shilling should not be lost by the great body of Dickens' admirers. The MS. is clearly printed on good paper,though it is far from easy to read. The author's erasions,alterations,and corrections are plentiful, and quite as much an at- traction and study as they are a drawback to a ready perusal of the work in its original form.— "Cassell's Magazine "commences the new year aus- piciously with an excellent number. Noticeable amongst its enjoyable contents is a story by Bret Harte. entitled "See Yap." It is of the heathen Chinee sort, and told in a most amusing style. A cute Celestial—probably endowed with the smile which is childlike and bland to a large ex- tent—carries through a double transaction by sel- ling a plot of psuedo-auriferoua land, and selling its purchasers, some high and mighty miners very anxious to sell him. A case of " the biter bit" was never more humorously told. A new volume is commenced by the January number of " Little Folks," which presents an extraordinary budget of allurements for its juvenile readers. Given away with the num- ber is a3O page album of "Simple songs for Little Folks," set to charming music. Among the splen- did prizes offered in various competitions are a bicycle, silver watches and skates. There are three serials all written in a vein which cannot fail to in- terest and amuse to the fullest possible extent. The general contents are as varied and attractive as ever.--A special enlargement is made of "The Magazine of Art," in order to give due prominence to an article on "The Face of Christ," by :•-ir Wyke Bayliss, P.R.8.A., F.S.A. It is described as a pain- ter's study of the likeness from the time of the apostles to the present day, and the article is illus- trated by 24 reproductions of the portraits of Christ as conceived by the old and new masters. Some of Use pictures are from paintings by Bellini, Fra Angelico, Van Evck, Michael Angelo, Titian, Raphael, Da Vinci, Velasquez, Vandyck, Holman Hunt, &c.

SMITH, ELDER AND Co.—Readers of " Cornhill " cannot complain of their favourite periodical being at all backward with new features for 1898. "Fights for the Flag," is the title of a series of stir- ring military stories, the first of which by the Rev. W. H. Titchett, is "Sir John Moore at Commas." There is quite a war-like tone about the current issue of "Cornhill,"' for in addition to the above, there is a contemporary letter on " Waterloo," and also a ballad well written, and of a sanguinary hue,by Conan Doyle. Items of a different charac- ter and more in the nature of romance are "The Strange Story of Madame Lefarge," "The Threlkeld Ear," and " Drumlochty Baccilus," while " A Liter- ary Friendship," and an anniversary study of " The poetry of Byron," save the book from becoming a mere collection of sensational matter. The contents, on the whole, are nicely diversified and promise well for the corning year, with a new serial by Stanley Weyman, entitled "The Castle Inn." Afacatir.Lars AND Co.—Of the Illustrated Stan- dard Novels the last to hand is " Northanger Ab- bey," and " Persuasion " by Jane Austen. Austin Dobson writes the introduction which prefaces this,as it does all the (Aber books of the series, and relates the somewhat adventurous history of the original M.S. of" Northanger Abbey " previous to its first publication. Illustrations capitally poor- traying the events of the story and the characteris- tics of people of the period,are present in consider- able numbers, from the pen of ffub Thompson.— Philip 11. of Spain" is the recent issue in the Foreign Statesmen Series. It is edited and com- piled by Martin A. S. Hume.—" The Century," "Macmillan's," and" St. Nicholas "for the current month are all replete in items of the highest class of general literature. WARD, LOCK, AND Co.—Extraordinary value for the money is the January number of "The Windsor Magazine." Two new series of stories commence. "Jenny Baxter, Journalist," is introduced to the readers in an audacious manner,and Percy Andrea's gentleman adventurer "T ;,e Emperor's Detective," is ushered in with considerable éclat and bravado. We confidently anticipate some good reading in both these series. The magazine also makes a hit with its n.tisr "Parliamentary Portfolio." The pic- tures are from the pencil of Sir J. P. Stone, M.P., whose pertinacity and skill have enabled him to present our great men in the most natural manner possible. Sven Hedin, a daring young Swede,and the latest explorer to astonish the world, tells a narra- tive of hardships in the East, which compels the close perusal of all.—Messrs. Ward Lock and Co. are issuing a new series of Penny Books for the People which, as regards both careful editing and quantity, and quality of printing and paper, will surpass anything of the kind that has yet been pub- lished. NEWNEB AND Co.—" The Strand Magazine," this month well sustains the promise given in the Christmas double number that im- provements and attractions were to be con- stant.—" Woman's Life" still keeps to the front in the ranks of penny periodicals. CONSTABLE AND CO.—One of the most charming children's gift books we have ever seen it "A llouse- ful of Rebels" by .Walter Rhoades. It is an account of three naughty girls and their adventures in fairy- land. Little boys and girls, the latter especially must simply gloat over this delightful "fairy tale,'" which is suitably illustrated. A handsome binding encloses the whole,and the published price is 4s. 6d. —Mr. George Lawrence Gomme's lectures on "The Principles of Local Government," which were de- livered at the London School of Economics,last year, have just been published in book form, and make a volume exceeding 200 pages. The author's aim has been to set, forth the lines upon which the principles ; of local government should be studied. At present i principles of local government, he says, are not n this country considered at all. "There is a vague I sort of idea that local government is a good thing for Parliament to occupy itself with, but there is no attempt to consider it as a subject which is governed by principles and not by fancy, which should not therefore be left to the sudden energy of Parliaments desiring to be busy with something new. . . lam emboldened to hop:, that all my research and all my experience as a local govern- ment official may have produced results which, if not conclusive, will lead to their being made so by others more fitted for the heavy task than I can hope to be. In the meantime,l hope to continue these lec- tures with a view of elalsorating many of the more important subjects which have been so briefly sur- veyed in the present series." We can safely recom- mens the work as one calculated to be of immense assistance and value to all connected with or in- terested in local government and its principles. Mr. Gomme, s arguments are concisely stated and clearly expounded, with logic unmistakably as ' their basic. The volume contains an analysis of contents, an index, and copious illustrative notes. The price is 12.3.—A work of considerable historical value is " The Pupils of Peter the Great," (Ids.), a history of the Russian Court and Empire from 1697 to 1740, by P. Nisbet Bain. The author follows out ! step by step, the early development of the modern Russian state from the latter years of Peter the Great to the death of his niece, the Tsaritsa Anne, and also draws a picture of the imperial court as it struck contemporary observers. The period chosen, embraces,roughly speaking, the first 40 years of the 15th century, which contains very largely the his- tory of the Russian Court. There is ample food for consideration and study in the work for serious political and historial students. Moreover tse singu- larly eventful history of the Russian Court alone I makes its investigation more than ordinarily inter- esting. The compilation of the text has been a task of great magnitude and Mr. Bain has spared no pains to secure the highest authorities, and verify his statements. There is nothing prosy about the work, which renders its study an easier matter than might be expected. It is almost superfluous to add that M essrs. Constable have allowed no opening for iticism in the way the book is placed before the public. A strong binding, thick paper, and large type are characteristics of editions from their press.

Adelaide, the scene of the third and most interesting of the test matches, is the moat Evangelical of the Aus- tralian capitals,and is not unfrequently referred to,in an ironical sense, by its less religious neighbours as ths " holy city.' It was there that the attempt to lure ligious folk into the theatre to witn^ss "The Sign of the Cross " was so vigorously denounced from the pulpit. Sir George Grey, who was Governor at Adelaide in 1841, when the city was in its infancy,still survives in our midst. He bound the plac" practically losokrupt, with the bills of his predecosaor dishonoured, but he succeeded in rescuing the little colony from the slough of deapond, and placing it on its financial legs again.

ALLEGED THEFT OF MONEY AT DARESBORY. At the Warrington Occasional Court on Thursday morning, before Mr. James Tinsley, James Shaw, was charged with stealing £3 12. in money and °Vbs. of tobacco, the property of Charles Jones, Dare-bury, on the 12th inst.—Ann Jones said she saw the money all right at eleven o'clock, on the previous day, and at 12 o'clock it was gone. She waited until her husband came in and told him about it. P.C. Robinson, of Moore, spoke to hav- ing apprehended the prisoner. It appears that the officer saw the man in a 'public-house, and his sus- picions being aroused he searched him and found the money and tobacco on him.—Prisoner was re- manded for eight days. INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY—WARRINGTON BRANCH.

Mr. i. Ridley, of t—he Liverpool Fabian Society, opened the second series of Sunday evening lec- tures held under the auspices of the above party, in the Druid's Hall, on Sunday evening, Mr, C. B. Brown, president of the Chester Trades and Labour Council,occupied the chair. " The title of the lecture was" Some mistakes about Socialism." These tnis- takes,or objections, it was pointed out, might be roughly divided into two sections. The first con- sisted of a number of statements which usually showed the objector to be either ignorant of the essential principles of Socialism, or stupid, bigoted, or prejudiced. The second class of objections,usu ally made by earnest, thoughtful men, seeking after truth, and which always gave the lecturer great pleasure in answering, mainly arose from miscon- ception of the nature of the aims and the work which Socialism would accomplish. The lecturer having stated these objections proceeded to answer them in a persuasive manner, closing with an ap- peal for a more studious and thoughtful considera- tion of Socialism. A number of questions were answered and the meeting closed by singing Edward Carpenter's noted hymn "England Arise."

At the meeting ef the Derbyshire County Council on Wednesday the Asylum Committee asked for power to extend the present county asylum at Mickleover se as to provide accommodation for 750 patients, as against 600 at present. The cost of the extension was eitimated at £21,000. It was admitted, howovet, that more money would be required shortly if lunacy were to continue to Increase at the rate of the past ten years. The matter was, after discussion, referred back to the Committee.

THE DISTRESS IN IRELAND. A crowded meeting of the local branch of the Irish National League was held in the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday. The President, Mr.J. C. • Dalton, presided, and was supported by Mr. J. C. Roddy, V.P. ; Thomas Hughes, secretary; Thomas Kilgallon, treasurer; Professor Ronan. Brian Kelly, , and others. The Chairman said they had met that , day in order to elicit sympathy of a practical , nature on behalf of their starving brethren in Ire- land. They were doubtless aware that the Mayor , had kindly convened a meeting at the Town Hall, for the purpose of opening a subscription on behalf of the sufferers. The Irishmen had been challenged to do their duty to their own kith and kin on that sad occasion.and though they were poor,they would nobly do their duty. The President then read Professor Long's articles describing the sufferings of the poor Irish in County Mayo. The meeting was also addressed by Professor Ronan, Mr. Roddy, Mr. Lane, Mr. Kelly, and Mr. Harvey. A subscription list was opened, and a good start was made by the audience subscribing sums amounting to £8 las 3d. The annual election of officers then took place,with the following result :—President, Mr. J. C. Dalton; vice-president, Mr. Charles Roddy ; secretary, Mr. , Thomas Hughes; assistant secretary,Mr.M. Mahon ; treasurer, Mr. Thomas lilgallon ; registration secre- taries, Mr. Patrick Hession and Mr. Frayne ; com- mittee, Messrs. Lane. BEAR THIS IN MIND.

A " Cnid "is not necfsrily the result of a high or low temperature. A person may go directly from a hot bath directly into a cold one, or into snow even,and not take cold. Re may remain out in the coldest atmos- phere until chilled through, and still not take cold. On the contary, he may take cold by pouring, a couple of teaspoonfuls of water upon some part of his dress, or by standing in a door or before the stove, or by sitting near a window oi• other opening, where one part of the body IS colder than the other part. Let it be kept in mind that unifermity of temperature over the whole body is the great thing to be looked after. It is the unequal 1 heat upon different parts sf the body that produces Colds by disturbing uniform circulation of the blood, which in turn induces congestion of some part. Beep the feet always dry and warm, avoid draughts of air, keep the system braced up by plenty of sleep and good food and by a powerful Tonic, and you will be proof against a Cold and its resits. ALLEGED CHIID NEGLECT AT WARRINGTON. On Monday morning at the Warrington Bereugh Police Court, before Mr. T. Birchen (chairman), Mr. J. W. Hayward, Mr. S. Wright, Councillor; Seew Green and Smethurst, William Harper, 1, Bolton's-yard, Turner- street, wee summoned for neeleeling his child Mary, 11 years of age, oa divers dates during the past 6 months. —Mr. A. Bro eene appeared for the defence.—lnspector McAteer, N.S.P.O C., said on the Ist January he visited the defendant's house wile Sergeant Hollingworth. They found there his wife, who was an invalid, and con- fined to bed, and three other women. The child Mary, who was also there was insufficiently clothed, having only one garment, and her fiesta was very dirty, but her head was clean. The bed in one of the rooms upstairs was indescribable. The floor was covered from side to side with flock. There was no food in the house, and no fire with the exception of a few red cinders. They found the defendant with his son-in-law at 22 Haydack- street. He was sitting by the fire in a beastly state of drunkenne33. The left side of his face was covered with blood. When he was spoken to about hi 3 child he made ' no reply. He (witness) went again to the house on the 4th January. and the girl was in the same condition as before, and there was again no food in the house. The defendant as a file cutter, and he had ascertained that he earned 30e. a week. He said he would prove that the child had been grossly neglected for the past six weeks, that the defendant had only given his wife two or three shillings a week, and that he had been drunk every night for the past fortnight.— Servant Hdlingworth taid he visited the house with P.C. Lewis on the Ist January and saw the defendant there beastly drunk and making use of bad language. Ilia wife was on the bed beside the fire-place, and she seamed very low and weak. She complained that there was no food in the house,and that the shopkeeper would not let them have any more. He reported the case to the Inspector. He was shown a bottle of whiskey that wag taken out of the defendant's pocket,and his son and another young man had to drag him home.—Peter Harper, one of the defendant's sons, a young man 23 years of age, said he had been at home six weeks. He had not done much work, but had been looking after his mother. On the Ist inst. his mother sent him out about three o'clock tc look for his father. He came back and told her he could not find him. He afterwards saw his father at the bottom ef Turner-street with a woman and another man. He had a battle of whiskey in his pocket. He would not come home, but wanted to go with the woman, and he (witness) had to drag him home. Defendant could make more than 30e .a week.— In reply to Mr. Bro Rile, witness denied that food was taken out of tl-e house by some women, when the In- spector came.— Mt Browne,on behalf of the defendant, said the defendant's wages were from 21s to 22s a week, and not 30;,as had been stated. He worked at Thewlis's, and was not to be blamed at all. On the let January he brought several articles of food.but when the Inspec- tor came they were tasen out of th 3 house by some women. Directly the officers were gone the food was broaght back again. On the day before Christmas the defendant gave his wife 181. He complained that his wife encouraged Peter not to work, and that she :vented him to it by the fire reading "Comic Cuts." She herself was suffering from paralysis brought on by ale-- holism. —Sarah Tatlock, a married daughter, said the child was not in want of. food.—James Harper, another of defendant's sons, said the child would not keep herself clean. His father always turned is money up,and he had not been drunk every night for a fort- night as was stated. The best part of the money that his mother received went for drink. Defendant only got drink on Saturday nights.—Mrs. Gibbons, defendant's sister, having given evidence in support of defendant, the Beach retired for deliberation, and on their return the Chairman (Mr. Bireball) said the evidence lid not warrant a charge of cruelty to the child. Still, they thought the Inspector was justifie i in bringing the case to the court. The condition of the home was certainly deplorable,and it was very sad to know there was such homes in Warrington. He was afraid they could only come to the conclusion that there was drunkenness on the part of bath father and mother. He hoped the In- spector would keep his eye on the case,and no doubt if it developed to anything worse they would hear more of it. The case would be dismissed.

THE DISHONESTY OF 'Bus CONDUCTORS. On Wednesday morning at the Warrington Police Court, before Mr. John Fairclough (chairman), Mr. J. Geddes and Mr. T. Birchall, Arthur Winstanley, a youth of about 17, was charged with embezzling the sum of 4d., the money of his master, Edward Conner.—He pleaded guilty.—Mr. Browne prosecuted, and said that the sum seemed small, :Jut the difficulty was to get a case to justify a conviction. Ho understood that Mr. Connor bad been robbed to the extent of £5 or £6 a week by different conductors. Be had let off no less than 12 who had been caught. Now he was bound to prosecute for his own protection. On• the day of the theft Mr. Connor's son went to Latchford to" check," and joined defendant's bus at Wash-lane. He looked at the sheet, and found not a single passenger marked off though there were 7 on the bus then. Defendant said be bad no passengers on top going out, but Mr. Connor as- certained that there were seven people en the top on the outward journey. When taken to the office defendant admitted his guilt, and nulled out 1. 93. and said that was what he had lost and rcbbed Mr. Connor of.—Mr. William Connor said they had determined to prosecute in every case in future, of both rlrivers and conductors. Previously they had let off 12 conductors bacause of in- sufficient evidence and from other reasens.—The Bench severely admonished the defendant, who seemed very repentant, and be was let off with a conviction re- corded against him.

Bewsey-street, Warrington not later than 10 o'clockon Thursday morning or they cannot be inserted. RUNCORN'S Ems CH SUCCESSIVE DUEAT.

For several weeks Runcorn have been down in the " dumps " and it was thought in the previous week that this feeling had about passed over and that things would probably begin to look up again. Saturday Cams but Runcorn were still minus some of their shining lights, and good, but inexperienced lads had to fill the vacan- cies. There was no lack of interest i a the encounter on Saturday, because Leigh who pessessed three Run- cornians were the visiting team. Toe entrance to Wil- son's Hotel was crowded long before there were any signs of the Lancashire men nearing the port. When they arrived they were lustily cheered and many went to the Dunbavins and Molyneux and warmly welcomed them into their native town. The visitors were all big men and appeared in the finest oficonditioe.Runcorn who had neither Butterworth, Reece, nor Robinson on, were very poorly represented. The ground, particularly the grandstand side,was in a wretched condition,not a blade' of grass being near. Something ought to be done before the season is out to remedy this as in damp weather the loose soil becomes wet and generally before the game has been going half an hour the players get mauled and rubbed in this slush and are simply unrecognisable. The teams on entering the arena had a warm reception and soon got to work. The following were the teams : Runcorn : Houghton, full hack; Hail wood, Nickson. ' Lightfoot, Williamson, threcquarter backs ; J. Faulkner and Jolley, half backs ; Darlington, Antrobus, Walker, Bettley, Moores, Jarneeon, Farmer, and Lang, forwards. Leigh : Coop, full back. Hodgenn,Pearson, Anderton, 0. Dunleavin,threequarter backs ; Molyneaux and J.H. Dunbavin half backs • P. Taylor, R. Taylor, Eccleston, Ridge, Boardman, E. France, Cheetham, and McMas- ters, forwards. Leigh won the tosa and Antrobue started operations for Runcorn, a poor return being made. The game be- came fast and exciting and owing to a Leigh forward mulling the ball, the hornescers were let in their oppon- ents' quarters. Here the scrimmages were frequent, and H. Dunbavin handled the ball and attempted to make an opening, but was soon floored. Runcorn assumed the aggressive and tackled spl ndidly. By a vigorous rush the visiting forwards led by Boardman, made their way to fresh quarters and carried play to centre before they were stopped. A free kick was given to Leigh and this enabled them to get their first foothold in Runcorn's territory. Their stay was not of long duration for Jolley behind the scrimmage passed to his partner who punted well down the field. Coop saved his side consi- derably by a long shot. I little later the home full hack had to kick out and this he did splendidly with his accustomed ceelness. Exchange kicking was the order for a few minutes until Leigh made an effort to gain ground in their opponents' quarters, but Houghton made them retrace their steps to the half way line. From here Leigh made desperate efforts to do something great, but the home forwards defended well, and kept their oppo- nents on the half-way lima for some minutes. 4 second later saw both teams on the home lines, Runcorn battl- ing hard to prevent disaster. The Lancastrians kept the home men penned in and were several times exceedingly dangerous, but the Cestrians probably aware of the weak nature of their side, kicked the ball dead. This was the safest thing for them to have done under the circumstances, as otherwise a try must have in- evitable been gained. From the kick the oval tra- velled into touch and shortly afterwards Leigh were seen racing up the field. H. Dunbavin started a passing movement. He was 'pecked up by Molyneux, Hodgson, Pearson, Anderton, and 0. Dunhavin, and all went nicely until Anderton passed to three-quarter. Dunbevin who was slow and failed to take the pass. Toe homesters broke in,and had matters for a short time a little their own way, but Leigh put on more steam and merged into the Cestrians 25 and scrimmages were very frequently ordered to take place in close proxmitv to goal posts. The heeling out of Runcorn was grand, and the danger was soon cleared. Anderton by a flying kick sent to Houghton but the latter was pushed in touch at the corner flag before he had time to do anything. H. Dunhavin was afterwards seen making headway with Hodocn at his heels. The former was about to be tackled and he cave a pass to the latter who ran over comfort- ably. The kick at goal was unsuccessful. Ruocorn made a determined effort to equalize, and Coop who was at sea on many occasions touched down. Neutral territory was then t e scene of hostilities for a little time. Jolley from a scrimmage passed to Faulkner, the latter on to Nickson, and then on to Lightfoot, but the latter let the ball go. At half time the visitors led by a try to nil.

On changing ends Leigh went with more dash and vigour and carried operations to the homesters 25. Moly neux here landed a goal hem an easy place. Leigh made several more determined efforts to score. and Molyneux wha was doing a lot of dodging, was grounded and the ball was afterwards taken to midfield by Faulkner. Leigh continued their efforts to score and a passing movement initiated by Molyneux brought his men in closer contaet with the hema line. A pass to Hodgson was not taken, the latter slipping, and Runcorn rushed up and play was again in the Lancashire men's territory A scrimmage was ordered and for some informality on the part of one of the vistors, Runcorn were gran ed a free kirk. 411'• A LOCAL FARM BAILIFF FINED FOR

KILLING A HARE ON CHRISTMAS DAY.

AN OLD-EN-ACTI4ENT.

Christopher Robinson, farm bailiff for Mr. Comber at Middleton, near Warrington, was charged at the instance of Mr. J. W. Knowles, solicitor, at Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday, with having on Christ- mas Day unlawfully killed game. Mr. Swift, solicitor, prosecuted on behalf of Mr. Knowles, and Mr. Arthurßrowne, solicitor, dsfended. In opening the case, Mr. Swift stated that Mr. Knowles was the occupier of some land at Middle- ton, sarrounding the house at which he lived, and the defendant was the farm bailiff to Mr. Comber, who was the occupier of Middleton Hall Farm. Oa Christmas pay"the defendant in company with another man :was seen by Mr. Knowles welkin 2 along the fence which divided the land of the two parties. Mr. KnoWle was looking through his bedroom window when he saw the defendant fire a shot at what appeared to Ix 3 a hare. A second shot was fired, and Mr. Knowles then Saw AshleV, whs was in company with the defend- ant, go up to the dividing fence for the hare. Mr. Knowles was unable to say whether the hare was taken off the land over which he had shooting rights, or whether it was on Mr. Comber's land. The defendant went away and Mr. Knowles immediately proceeded to the spot where he imagined the hare fell, and saw dis- t;nct trac.!s of the fur which had ben shot off the hare, on his shooting land. There were also shot marks on the fence. Later in the day Mr. Knowles saw the defendant, and (Mr. Knowles's) land, and Robinson replied that it Mr. Knowles charged had aco with hare on himw ithhnavveirnsga tischnot was shot on their land. The shots through the hedge seemed to point to the hare having ben on Mr. Knowles's land,but he was unable to prove it,and there- fore he simply took out a summons chaiging the defend- ant with shooting on Christmas Day. Mr. Browne would no doubt refer to the old Act under which the proceed- ings were taken, and argue that it was inoperative for these enlightened times. The proceedings were taken under the Gaming Act of 1.831,but even in the amended Act of 1880 it was expreaily provided that the exemP- tions for shooting should be included in the amended Act.

Mr. J. W. Knowles gave evidence in support of Mr. Swift's statement, He Btated that he heard a hare cry, but he c^,uld n)t conscientiously say on his own oath on which side of the fence the hare was at at the time. After it was shot Ashley went to fetch it, and he was down by the fence for nearly a minute. The fence looked as if Ashley had had his body through. The fur on the field also simesteil chat the hare had been shot on his (witness's) shooting land which was occupied by Mr. Swinton.

Mr. Arthur Browne for the defence said the defendant was eloarfed with the extraordinary offence of killing a hare on Christmas Day. The section of the Act under whieh the proceedings were taken was passed so far back as 1831, and by it a person was liable to a penalty for shootinz a hare on Chris'm is Day, hot not in Case he shot a rabbit. tLsughter.) There was therefore this extraordinary difference that it was right to shoot a rabbit on Christmas Day but not a hare. flis friend, Mr. Knswles was the common informer in that case, and most he treated as such. He came to that ceurt charting the defendant with having killed a hare on Christmas D 1.% under the belief that it was shot on his land. But the defendant's word and that of his teamstnan must hs nonsidered quite as good as Mr. Knowles'. Mr. Robinson told him that the hare was shot on Mr. Comber's land and with his permission. Mr. Knowles must have ne of three objects in view in bringing thet case before the court. He must be aotuated by a kind humanitarian spirit, or a Christian spirit with the object of keeping up the morals of his district, or personal re- VPIIZP against the defendant. Re (Mr. Browne) sug- g,sted that the oniv reason for the prosecution was re- venge. It was a paltry, mean, common spirit on the part of one person towards his neighbour. and utterly nevond the charitable instincts of Mr. Knowles in his ordinary course of life. But for this feeling on the part of Mr. Knowles he ventured to say that they would never have heard of this case. Mr. Knowles was equally en- titled under this act to summon any poor fish dealer from Warrington or elsewhere for selling mackerel on Sunday. With regard to the shooting of•the hare both the defendant and Ashley who accompanied, him would swear that it was shot on Mr. Comber's land, and that they were ignorant of it being an offence to shoot a hare on Christmas day, He (Mr. Browne).must admit that he did not know it was an offence himself. His friend Mr. Knowles had gone Bo far back as 1831 to fish nut the only possible conviction he could get, simply with the idea of revenge. This was a case which raised the ire of commonsense Englishmen to think of, and he ventured to predict that if the magistrates used the powers they had under the Act it would go through the country that they were trying to keep up an old law, which was more honoured in the breach than in the observance. He asked the magietrates to deal with the case under the powers they pr wafted by an Act passed by Lord Cross enabling them to di4pose of cases such as the present.

The Chairman 01,..Gillespie)saicl no dopht a technical offence i:een commiqed, and the magiatrates had decided to flue the defendant 2s. ad. including costs.

1.-A TERRIBLE BOILER EXPLOSION.

ON a Sunday morning three years ago—viz., February 10th, 1895 (to quote from the "Putney and Wandsworth News), a loud explosion occurred in the kitchen of a house in Putney. A constable found that the boiler had explodei, wrecking the room and its contents. On the stairs near by, Miss White Was found lying in an ex- hausted and insen.ible condition. A doctor was called, and ordered her removal to the Westminster Hospital, wfisre she was conveyed in a cab, and found to be suf- fering from severe contusions and burns,and from shock to the system, and detained in hospital. In the explosion the wall was blown, clean down ; the ceiling was stripped, the doors driven in, shelves, chairs, crockery, and in fact, everything in the room utterly wrecked. cat sleeping in a chair was instantly killed. Miss White's escape from death certainly appears mira- culous. A few days ago,she was pointed out to a news- paper man, to whom the account of her remarkable I escape, with some very interesting after-developments, was related.

" After I left the hospital," Miss White said, " I was prostrated with the shock to the system, and I lay for weeks between life and death' Even when I recovered from the accident I rapidly grew weaker, and was so debilitated that I couldn't wark without assistance, and I had fearful indigestion after eating, and often fainted. It was a terrible time for me, I can tell you. The doctors said I was anomie, and I got se white I was almost afraid to look at myself in the glass. My mother and father said I should never be the same girl again, but you see tkey were, happily, mistaken." And the pressman found this to be true as he turned his eyes to the bright, animated face of the speaker. " And to what do you attribute so complete a re- covery ?" he asked. Well, I will tell you. I w is sitting up ill in bed one morning reading the dritish Weekly, and I, saw an ac- count of the case of a young lady whose sufferings seemed just like mine. I decided to try the medicine that cured her—Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. We soon found that the Pills were doing me good. After taking them I felt like another being. The pains began to be less severe. I was less irritable. Palpitation of the heart, which had been very troublesome and alarm - ing, ceased. The change was simply wonderful. To make a long story short, I got well, and now I am as strong and healthy as ever I was in my life." The above statement can easily be verified. The Whites have resided in Putney over thirty years, and are known as highly trustworthy and respectable psople. Their address is " Ormesby," Fawe Park-road Putney.

ANNUAL OLD FOLK'S TitEAT AT WARRINGTON. On Thursday,last week,the annual treat to the old people of Warrington was given in the Parr Hall. The men were regaled with a substantial dinner of roast beetpotatoes,and carrots,with beer to drink,at 12 o'clock, and the women sat down to tea at 5.30. There was not quite so large an attendance of the old men at noon as in the previous year, the figures being respectively 30 and 430. This may be attri- buted in some measure to better trade. In conse- quence of the redaction in numbers the officials allowed twelve applicants, who had not attained the stipulated age of 60 years,to enter the Hall and share the bounpy provided. The oldest of the men present was one old fellow of 86 years. Dinner lasted an hour and a half, and during that time a large number of ladies and gentlemen worked hard as waiters, on the wants of the aged. Amsng those present were :—The Mayor (Alderman J. E. Wright), Alderman Sutton, Councillors Jolley,Smet- hurst, Lee, Cannel', the Revs. .E. C. E. Carleton, H.R. Wild, H. V. Pigot, and Father Folding, Messrs. J. C. Holmes, sen., G. Pilling, D. Fanshaw, D. Jones, W. Gill, G. Gill, T. J. Garnett, J. Harrison, J. Sampson, H. Holt, W. Litton, H. Ferguson, F. Strettle, T. A. Sutton, W. Sutton, P. Chrimes, H.Toft, G. Watkins, L. Talbot, I. Hayward, W. Roberts, H. Emson, W. Reid, and Pitt. The Mayoress (Mrs. Wright), and Mrs. James Fairclough were also present. Selections on the organ were played at intervals by Mr. F. Ward.

At the conclusion of the dinner each of the men who desired was given half aa ounce of tobacco and a clay pipe. Before despersitig, Alderman SUTTON addressed the old folks, saying that they would now have the usual bhort toasts and part good friends. First of all he would aoc them to drink the toast thay never forgot, and that was " The health and happiness of Her Majesty the Queen."—The toast was drunk with heartiness and three rousing cheers for Her Majesty followed. Alderman SUTTON, continuing, said that having drunk the health of the highest lady in the land he would ask them now to drink the health of the highest lady and gentleman in the room' the Mayor and Mayoress is Applause.) They would all be aware that the Mayor had been on the committee of this Institution for a long number of years, and not only so, but he had taken a very warm interest in everything connected with it,and they ought to congratulate him and themselves that he was now ths leading magistrate of the Borough. (Ap- plause.) They all hoped that he would enjoy good health and happiness during the term of his office, be- cause they knew that there was much to do in connec- tion with the Mayoralty and little spare time for any- thing else ; they also trusted that he would fulfil his duties with satisfaction to himself and the Borough.— (Applause).—Hearty cheers followed the toast and The MAYOR, in responding, expre3sed the pleasure it was to him to meet them again and see them all enjoy themselves,as they had that day. So far as he was con- cerned he felt his responsibility in the high position of Mayor of Warrington very much, and if it pleased God to spare him health and strength he would do his duty in whatever station of life he was placed, and he hoped to their satisfaction. He could hardly tell them how much he was interested in this Institution, which was founded about 40 years ago, but he could say that it gave the committee and officers greater pleasure to pro- vide the treat than it did the old folks to receive it. (Applause.) They looked forward to this day with feelinas of pleasure. He himself was pleased to have the opportunity of wishing them all a happy and pros- perous New Year. (Loud applause.) A number of comic songs etc., were rendered until 2 o'clock, and the men then despersed. • . .

The treat to the aid women was given at 5.30, when 553 were seated round the tables. An appetising meal of ham, beef, and mutton sandwiches, meat pies, mince pies, tea and oranges was provided. The lady who laid claim to being the oldest present was Susannah David- son, 94 years, who was exceptionally hale and hearty. Next to her came 3 at 85, one at 81, 2 at 83. Then there was a long fall until 70 years was reached, and of this age there were 34. There were 44 at 64, and 44 at 65 years. Thirty seven under 60 years were admitted, there being accommodation, and all those admitted too- ling widows with large families. There were about 200 ess than in the previous year at the women's tea. Taking the combined numbers of men and women who partook of the treat, it was found that there was a decrease of 350 on last year. Commencing at 7-30 a miscellaneous entertainment was given in Parr Hall.when the majority of the women remained seated round the tables in the body of the hall, and a large number were assembled in the back gallery. The large building was crowded in every part, and the heat was stifling. Comic songs were given by Mr W H ForShaw,Mr.Gray, Mr. J. Emson, Mr. Fiddler humorous sketches by Mr. Muirhead,Mr.Bell and songs by Miss May Pierpoint,Mrs. R. H, Pearson, and Mr. W. Bellian. A ventriloquial en- tertainment was given by Mr. A. Grounds. All the items were well received, and the entertainment appeared pro- ductive of enjoyment to those present. It should be mentioned that Councillor Alfred Woods discharged the onerous duties of ticket-collector and age-recorder during the whole of the day. Th officials of the Old Folks 'rreat,who have so successfully carried the affair through are :—President, Alderman Thomas Sutton ; vice-president,Mr. Daniel Jones ; tion.treasurer, Mr. Peter Chrimes ; hon. secretaries, Alderman John E. Wright, and Mr. T. Arthur Sutton ; committee, Mr. D. Fanshaw,Mr. James Smethurst, Mr. George Pilling, Mr. Peter Caldwell, Mr. Alfred Wools, Mr. Harry Toft, Mr. Arthur Brown, Mr. W. Gill,and Mr. Charles Bunch.

IL-RAILWAY ENGIINE MAE'S ESCAPE.

It Wig (writes a " Mid-Surrey Gazette " reporter) in Park-grove, Battersea, London, that I found young Robert Masters—a good-looking youth of about eigh- teen summers, strong, broad built, sturdy, and athletic. Had I seen him some few months earlier, he told me, I should have found him very different. He had had a narrow escape. "I was working as a plumber's mate," ha said, "and got wet through three times a week. I was laid up with pains in the head, and had a very bad throat. My mother got me some medicine, but it did not appear to do me any good. I had been laid up two weeks, and, thinking it was time I was better, I went out. I hadn't been out long wlym I completely lost the use of my legs and feet, and had to be carried home. I was put to bed, and next morning found myself covered with large red spots, some of which broke out into sores.

"Besides this, I had dreadful pains in my stomach, which nothing seemed to relive. The doctor described my illness as anemic rheumatism, and said I was in for a long illness. My mother had mentioned my illness to a lady friend, who told her that her son had been cured of rheumatics by taking Dr. Williams' Pink Fills for Pale People, and she told my sister of the circumstances. But, continued Mr. Masters, " my sister, who is always very prompt and decided in her actions, immediately put on her hat and jacket and scoured the chemists' shops till she got the genuine Pills." The effect was so rapid that in three days Mr. Mas- ters was up and dressed, and in a fortnight he was able to go out for a walk. In six weeks he had taken two boxes of the Pills and felt himself so completely cured that he applied for work at the Brighton Company's works in Victoria-road, and was at once taken on as an engine-cleaner, where he still remains, hearty and strong._

Mr. Henry Peers, with his bronzeed, healthy-looking face, grey moustache, and soldierly appearance, is now fifty-three years of age,and has served twenty-three and a half years in the Royal Navy. Be lives at Wednes- field, near Wolverhampton, served at the bombardment of Alexandria, and in Suakin, and holds the medals. At the close of the campaign he came home invalided. LANCASHIRE AND YORKSHIRE BANE. LIMITED. The report of the directors of this Bank to be presented to the shareholders on Tuesday next states that " During the past year the remaining 5,000 shares of the original capital of the Bank have been issued and allotted in terms of the circular to the shareholders dated Ist Of September.lB97. On these shares .£lO per share have been paid as capi- tal. making the total paid-up capital of the Bank .£500,000. The directors have paid the premium on this issue, .£100,000,t0 the reserve fund. The an- nual statement of accounts for the year ending 31st December, 1897, shows that the net profits of the Bank, after providing for bad and doubtful :debts a nd rebate on bills current, amount to .£66,30.1 151. 101, with the balance of £3,506 2s. Id brought forward from the previous year making a total of £69,811 7s. lid. An in- terim dividend of 10s. per share was paid in July. The directors now propose to pay a further dividend of 149. per share for the half-year ending 31st December,lB97, being a total distribution of 12 per cent, for the year, clear of income tax. They also propose to write off £5,000 from Bank Property Account, to tvld .£5,000 to the reserve fund, and to carry forward .£5,811 7s. lid., to the profit and loss new account. Mr. J. H. S. Crompton, who has been associated with the Bank since its commencement, having expressed a desire to retire from the Man- agement, the directors have accepted his resigna- tion with regret. They have appointed Mr. Thomas B, Maxon (formerly of Manchester and County Bank, Limited) as General Manager of the Bank, and Mr. Charles Lumsden, who has hitherto acted as Sub-Manager,has been appointed Manager. The directors retiring by rotation era Mr. W. B. Melland, Colonel O. O. Walker, and Mr. Henry Whitehead, all of whom will be proposed for re- election. The auditor, Mr. David Smith, F.C.A., re- tires, and offers himself for re-election. The divi- dend warrants will ba forwarded to the Share- holders on the 18th instant."

PROFESSO R MAX MiJLLE R'S RELIGIOUS BELIEFS.

A representative of the "Christian World " has been interviewing Professor Max Muller on the subject of his religious beliefs. He believes is one revelation only,the revelation within us. " Why should we look for God, and listen for His voice outside us only, and not within us ?" That inward voice never allowed him the slight- est doubt or misgiving about the reality of a future life. if there was continuity in the world everywhere, why should there be a wrench and annihilation only with us? Personally, he had no doubt of the persistence of the individual after death. From subsequeat observations the interviewer gathered that Profeszor Max Muller does not believe in a one-legged immortality; he believes just as strongly in the existence of the individual before birth into this world as in his continual existence when he goes out of it. Asked whether he considered the Bible insoired or that it contained a revelation in a sense different from that of any other writing, the Professor replied So far as the Christian Scriptures contain the doctrines of Christ they are inspired by Christ, and inasmulh as Christ stands much higher than the ancient sages and prophets, of course they are inspired in a higher sense." He went on to add ;—" When the Old Testament was no longer the sacred book of the early Christians, the mothers wanted something in its place to teach their children ' • and if there is a demand there is sure to be a supply. So different families began ti write down what they had heard from Matthew, Mark, John, and others. Our gospels are not the gospels of, but aEcording to Matthew, Mark,Jahn. What they believed St. John to have told them,that they wrote down." This method opened the door to understanding and error. THE IMMACULATE CONCEPTION.

The Prufessor explained in that way the account of the miraculous method of Christ's entry into, and depar- tute from, the world. The ordinary way of being born as thcught not to be good enough for the Great Teacher. In every religion there was the same desire for a pure birth, as if there could be an impure birth Is if there could ever be a maculate conception ! If it was not maculate in the eyes of thk Author of all things, why should it be so in ours ? To talk of an immaculate conception was simply an insult to the Author of our existence. And so with the idea of a bodily ascension. First of all, it was not mentioned in all the Gospels. Such an event, had it happened, could not have been , overlooked, for all the laws of gravity would have been suddenly broken and the whole world have trembled. But what can be more beautiful than a return to His Father in Heaven? What more groteeque than an as- cent, a flight through the clouds, and arrival at some corner of the blue sky? Asked if, then, Christ was only a man like unto ouiselves, the Professor gave an em- phatic answer in the affirmative. "What," he asked, "for us can there be higher than a man? Angels we have never seen, nor anyth"ng higher than man. That is what Christ Himself has taught us; He calls us His brothers and the sons of the same Father. What can be higher. He does not claim for Himself a nature differ- ent from ours. Take His own account of Himself : • I go to my Father and your Father. I and the Father are one.' We must net make Him contradict Himself, though no doubt there are slight contradictions in the gospels, as we have them. Therein is the advantage of studying other religions; we learn how such contradic- tions arise without anything fraudulent in them."

A BELIRVIEB IN NiERNAL If any reader is concerned about Professor Max

Muller's orthodoxy, let him reassurred by ths fact that the savant is a firm believer in eternal punishment. I have always held that it would be a miserable universe without eternal punishment. Every act, good or evil, must carry its consequences, and the fact that our punishment will go on for ever seems to me to be a proof of the everlasting love of God. Punishment may vary greatly in its nature ; there may be a great deal of love in punishment ; there certainly is In the punkt. ment which our deeds bring upon us. For any evil deed to go unpunished wowld be to destroy the moral order of the universe. Just as the stone thrown into the water goes on for ever drawing circles, so it ie natural to suppose we shall always remain under the in- fluence of our deeds. I should say the whole world would fall to pieces if we gave up the idea, in sense of the word, of eternal punishment, which, coming from God, must be eternal correction and eternal reward. With- out eternal punishment we should have no touch with God, the world would be Godless. God-forsaken.

THE RELIGION OF THE FUTURE. His ideas about the religion of the future, the Pro- fessor gave in these words :—" I hope it will become simpler and simpler. In every day life, when we want to know what to do, what to say, we do not need to think of Pontius Pilate or Barabbas ; we ought to think of one short sentence. " )ve your neighbours as self," That is very short, but it is as large as the world. The shorter a cried is the better and the tiller. We have Thirty•nine Articles, and hardly one of them COE. tains anything about religion. They contain plenty of theology—the result of compromises between different parties—but of religion, love of our neighbour, love of God, not a word. And those are called the articles of our faith ! Definite belief in specific doctrines is deci- dedly dwindling. For instance, if you get a man or woman by himself or herself, and ask, Do you really be- lieve that Christ went up through the stratum of clouds and arrived at a certain stai ? They are offended at the very suggestion. I have known a good many young men who have lived to become deans and bishops and archbishops and when they were young men they were just like others, rational and honest. I remember one of them speaking with scorn of the idea of physical miracles and it was very creditable to him. He is ROW a great bishop; I do not know whether he has changed his mind. Ido not think as. A great deal depends upon the way thing is said. An eminent Divine declared from the pal- pit before the University that belief in miracles wag henceforth impossible, but the real teaching of Christ would remain unshaken.

The recent judgment of the House of Lords has proved fatal in another trade union case, in which the County Court judge at Colas had awarded a non-unionist £lO. and costs against a stonemason at Nelson, who had bees the means of getting him and two others dismissed. Its an appeal before Justices Day and Lawrance on Tuesday It was argued that after the decision in the case of ...111en v. Flood" the court was bound to reverse this judgment. The court allowed the appeal, and entered judgment for the defendant, but without costs, and directed that the plaintiff's widow should receive her Costs out of the :amount pild into court by the detendant.

P ENKET H.

OLD FoLas Tnaar.—On Friday night last week the annual treat to the old people of Piinketh and district was held in the Conservative Club. The funds for this moat praiseworthy object ire yearly subscribed by ladies and gentlemen of the parish, and the old folks have looked eagerly forward to the date on whicla they meet and talk together of bygone days. l'he invitations were almost all responded to, 73 sitting down to the substan- tial tea, prepared under the supervision of Mrs. J. C. Routledge, assisted by Mesdames J. J. Routledge, J. Johnson, Longshaw, Pownall, Mainley, Leo Foster, and Misses Sankey, L. Routledge ad M. Pownall. The gathering was presided over by Mr. J. C. Routledge who has officiated in the same position since the inauguration of the treat in 1887. After tea the tables were cleared of the tea things and replaced with plates of tobacco and matches, and with the aid of the long "churchwardens" the men, (and one or two of the old ladies also) srttled down to hear the excellent prof rarnme. The first song was" The Old Folks at Rome," which was effectively rendered by Miss Nellie Moss. Mr. A. Grounds was present and created much. merriment with his musical sketch " A Village Concert," and later on by his ex- tremely funny ventrilcquial performance for which he received well deserved applaure. Miss London sang "Is yer mammy always yvid yer " and "Grandma's Advice," in a pretty manner, and though rather nervous came through in a sucmsful manner. Mr. W. Denton, always a favourite at this funetion,sang "Silly Tommy," ' and" That's where they found me," in a very funny style and was heartily applauded. Mr. J. Walters sang "The 'rise Schoolmaster," in a very taking manner the song being greatly appreciated by the old folks. Mr. A. Goulden sang "The Vacant Chair," and Mr. W. Pick. ton was to the fore with two very tastefully rendered accordeon solos. Mr. S. Davies gave two Lancashire sketches and Mr. G. Davies was deservedly encored for his song" The Love of Old." Mr. L. J, Forster was very succes4ful in his two humorous songs, and received a hearty round of applause. During the evening refresh- ments were passed round, the comfort of the old folks being looked alter in every passible way. " Auld Lang Sync," was the closing item, and the old folks shortly after dispersed, each receiviug a present, the women }lb of tea, and the men an ounce at tobacco. Those who where unable to be present had their present forwarded to them. A bui, was in attendance to convey those home who lived at a distance. The proceedings were efficiently carried through by the following committee r—Messrs. G. R 9 utlecige (chairman), Maiuley, Denton, Longsbaw Jackson, sal J. Fura:er (secretary).

THE IRISH DISTRESS FUND.

Sir,—l vrish it to be generally known to all those kind friends who have already Eui-)scribed through The Irish National Lague to the Mayor's fund, that it is our in• tention to forward all monies collected by Thursday the 20th inst, to Dr. Fox. A eompleta list of all subscribers will be duly published in the press. May I, through your kindness appeal to all Irishmen to do their best to swell our fund which is being sent to the General Fund in the name of the Irish National League. I will be glad to receive any amount, however small, which my countrymen may leave at my address.—Yours truly, J. C. DALTON, President, I.N.L. 136, Bridge-street, Warrington.
