A PUBLIC BOWLING GREEN FOR WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Allow me through the medium of your paper to suggest a way of utilising the Queen's Gardens. As the Bank Park does not afford sufficient space wherein a public bowling green could be provided without crippl- ling the recreation ground,or occupying a portion of the land fronting the Town Hall, I venture to name the Queen's Gardens as a suitable place where one or two greens might with advantage be laid. There are other towns which can boast of their public bowling greens, and judging from the way they are patronised by the public both as spectators and players, I see no reason why the Warrington public should not have this accom- modation afforded them. Will some other correspondent take the matter up ?—ln anticipation I beg to remain yours faithfully. Warrington, January 12th, 1898.

J. H.

THE CONDITION OF SNIIT:iFORD-ROAD. Sir,—May I ask that souse member of our Town Conn- -1(a Latchford Councillor preferred), should move a resolution at the nest meeting requesting the members of the Paving and Sewerage Committee, to walk in pro- eession some wet day from Warrington Bridge to Latch- ford Post Office and back. The procession might take the footpath for the journey out and walk back down the middle of the roadway—the Mayor at the head of the procession with the Borough Surveyor as professional accompanist. If this proposal is accepted and carried out and does not result in convincing the Committee which is responsible for the roads and streets of the town that Knutsford-road is in a most disgraceful condition, and demands immediate attention then I have not much hope of any , improvement for a long time to come. A considerable portion of the footpath is after every shower always under water, and there are hundreds of pools of various siz's in the roadway. If there is a main road as bad within 20 miles I should like to know where it is.—Yours truly, MAX.

SABBATH OBSERVANCE. Sir,—lf through any infirmity, literary or otherwise, we have failed to make our position in this controversy clear to your readers we are very sorry. We say this because one ,s 1 thorn at least continues to inaist that we are advocates of a human irstitution called Sunday. This charge we deny for the second and last time. We are contending for the inviolability of the Fourth Com- mandment as a part of the Moral Law which our Creator enacted for the government of the world. This Law oc- cupies a prominent place in the inspired writings ; and so far as we know has never been repealed or modified by its Divine Author. Your correspondent R.G.L. ap- pears to admit that nine out of the ten Commandments are still in force, but for Some reason or other, which he does not make plain, he would have us believe that the Fourth is no longer binding upon men. Now as the only authority who could rescind that or any other part of the Moral Law is God, we ask him when and where did God iescind the observance of the Fourth Commandment ? If he can point us to some clear and definite chapter of the ',lit:le where this repealing Act of Godis recorded we ci ddy admit that the position we have taken up is untenablc. Until then we must,with all due deference to our censor, continue to hold our pre- sent view. It is not only possible, but we think probable, that Jesus,either by direct verbal instruction,or by the secret operation of his spirit upon their hearts,may have com- municated His will to his disciples that henceforth the first day of the week,being the day of His resurrection from the dead,should be observed as the day of Sabbatic rest. At any rate we find that they met on the first day of the week to break bread in remembrance of His death. And when Paul was at Troas we read that the disciples came together on the first day of the week ; and the same apostle directed the Corinthian Christians to make provision for chiritable purposes at their meetings on the same day. Then again the Apostle John tells us that he was in the Spirit on the Lord's day when a series of wonderful revelations were made to him. And some fifty years after John's death Justin Martyr writs, "Oa the day called Sunday we meet together for reading the Scriptures and for prayer." All this stems to us to in- dicate that the observance of the day of rest had been changed,by the authority of Jesus, from the seventh to. , the first day of the week, that the two great events of the world, its creation and redemption, might be com- memorated at the same time. Be this as it may.there is certainly nothing in the recorded observance of the first day of the week to show us that the Divine law of the Sabbath had been abolished. And when we turn from the Scriptures to the world around us how strong is the testimony that is forth- coming in support of our contention that the Sabbath law is still binding upon men ! In all the countries that profess Christianity there are many and great differ- ences of opinion as to what Christianity really is, but there is one thing upon which most men are agreed, and that is that entire cessation from labour every seventh day is a solemn duty which they owe, not cnly to their Maker, but to themselves as frail and perishing crea- tures. And it gives us sincere pleasure to be able to state that this country is pre-eminent in this respect. In no 01 hr that we know of is the letter of the Sabbath law more honoured and obeyed than it is in this. When lae s,;yenth day comes round manufactoriea ef all kinds warehousel eho.ps, banlis,offices, !cc., are closed by nal. -non consent that the men and women who bear the heat and burdens of life may rest from their labours and, if so disposed, worship God according to the light He has given them. With this letter we close our share in this correspond- ence, and thank you, Mr. Editor, for the space you have so kindly given us for expressing our simple views on a subject which is undoubtedly of th- deepest importance to mankind in general, but especially to those who earn their bread by the sweat of their brow.—Yours truly, lONA.

THE TAWD VALE COLLIERY DISASTER. CHARITY CONCERTS AT WARRINGTON. Many people will have a keen recollection of the dis- aster which occurred in the 'lewd Vale, Skelmersdale, on November 13, by which two lives were lost and 800 men thrown out of employment. On this date the river Tawd broke into a disused mine and thence into the pits of the Tawd Vale Colliery Co.. filling the shafts to the level of the river in less than 24 hours. It was to aid the sufferers of this disaster that a comnaittee was formed to collect subscriptions, and in connection with this laudable object two sacred concerts were given in Parr Hall, Warrington on Sunday. Mr.E. Lloyd Jones is the secretary in Warrington and a circular issued by the committee makes an earneet appeal to the public on be- half of the sufferers. Already the pinching hand of poverty is keenly felt lathe homes where the bread- winners have been thrown out of work, the women and children feeling most of all the effects of this terrible dis- aster. Subscriptions 'may be sent to the treasurers, or to the Ormskirk or Skelmersdale branch of London and Midland Banking Co. In the afternoon Councillor Bennett occupied the chair and in the course of a brief address said he had heard objections raised in certain quarters to Sunday con- certs, but there were, unfortunately, a very large num- ber of people who never attended any place of worship, and it was better to listen to good music than to spend the day in mere idleness. To his thinking all music was sacred, but in any case no possible exception could be taken to the programme of that afternoon,and the music was sacred in the best sense of the word, as it was en- listed in the cause of charity. After alluding to the cir- cumstances which had brought them together, he said they were assembled with a definitely religious object. True religion undefiled before God and the Father was to visit the widows and the fatherless in their affliction, to help to bear each other's burdens, and so fulfil the law of Christ, and thus win the Master's commendation, "Inasmuch as ye have done it unto one of the least of these My brethren ye have done it unto Me." There was a fair attenuancs and songs were given by Miss Katie Thomas,Miss Bettie Mawdsley,Mr. H. Berry, Mr. W. EL Wallington violin aola by Miss Edith Bir- chen ; pianoforte solo by Miss C. K. Sault ; and a recita- tion, " The Leper," by Miss Kate Beesley. All the ar- tistes acquitted themselves to the general satisfaction of the audience,Miss flestie Mawdsley,however, coming in for chief honours. Her interpretation of " Angels ever bright and Fair," and " The Better Land," was almost Perfect and evoked very hearty applause. In the evening Mr. Henry Thornton presided,and there was a large attendance. The Chairman said he had known the people of Tawd Vale for upwards of thirty years; he knew exactlr what sort of people they were, and he had watched them in t .ieir circumstances during all those years. Be hoped the concerts would be as morally and spiritually profitable ae they could possibly be. It should in justice be mentioned that all the artistes gave their services free.and paid their own expenses. It Is to be hoped that their efforts were productive of a substantial lift being given to the relief fund.

NARROW ESCAPE PROM DROWNING.

On Monday afternoon a little boy named aughes living at 23, Vitriol-square, Earlestown, had a very narrow escape from drowniuk A girl named Dick- son had taken the child for a walk in the direction of the canal,and whilst playing on the swing bridge near Bradley Wood,the child fell into the water,and but for the plucky efforts of the girl, would have been drowned. The child was carried home, and after remedies had been applied it speedily re- covered.

THE PRISON GATE MISSION. The report ot Mr. C. W. Harris, the Missionary of the Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of the Diocesan C.E.T.S., for the month of December shows that he has made sixteen visits to the Wigan Borough, County, and Newton-le-Willows police courts, conversed with 60 persons in the courts and cells; and has made 44 visits to the abodes of per- sons whom he has met there. Five cases have been attended to in the courts at the request of the magistrates. One man has been sent by train to the Society's Firewood Factory at Bootle. Twenty- nine pledges have been taken. Ten meetings ad- dressed &c., &c. BUXTON'S CoNCERT PARTY AT EARLESTOwN.

Mr. Harry Buxton, who, with his choir, has for several years paid a visit to Earlestown, has during this week been delighting large audiences with his varied and high class entertainment. The concerted items are admirably rendered, and the individual singing is of excellent quality. A. most welcome introduction is the cinematograph, or animated pictures, which evokes considerable interest and applause, and is well worth a visit. Professor Fox causes much amusement with his ventriloquial en- tertainment, his manipulation of the figures being exceedingly clever. Mr. Charles Parker's entertain- ment, as a lightning change artist is wonderfully clever, and the other items are equally interesting, Two sacred concerts will be given in the Mission Hall on Sunday, and the party will bring their visit to a close on Monday night. HATDOCIT CRICKET AND BaWLING CLUB . .

SHOCKING BURNING ACCIDENT AT EARLESTOWN On Tuesday afternoon a child three years of age, a deaf mute, named May Whitby, daughter of a striker living in Bank-st , Earleotown, was shock- ingly burnt. Mrs. Whitby about three o'clock went into a neighbour's named Mrs. Nixon, the child accompanying her. She must however have re- turned to the house, as shortly afterwards Mrs. Nixon saw the child in the street in flames. The fire was extinguished as son as possible, and Dr. Mouncey was summoned i.nd applied the usual remedies, but the child was so badly burnt that no hopes were entertained of its recovery. It would appear that the child after leaving its mother had returned to the house and must have fallen against the bars of the fire grate, as the throat is badly scared. After suffering acute agony the unfortu. nate child died the same night. EARL STOWN AND DISTRICT FANCIERS' ASSOCIATION.

LIVELY ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual general meeting of the members of the above Association was held at the 0 leifellows' Arms, Earleeown, on Thursday evening. Mr. G. Payne was voted to the chair,and there was a moderate attendance of members.—Mr. Wightman said he thought Mr.Widd I (who was present) was in the wrong place.—Mr. Widd said he did not think ao,—Mr.Wighttnan said after what took place at the last meeting he thousht Mr. Widd had no right to be present at the meeting.—Mr. Widd said they had br eight a charge against him which was not correct.—Mr. Hallett said the matter was settled at the last meeting.—Mr. Widd read rule 21, which said that any member who was 12 months in arrear,or who should be guilty of any misconduct,auch misconduct should be submitted to a special meeting, and if such member be found guilty he should be expelled. He said the last meeting was an ordinary business meeting.—Mr.Wight- man said the last meeting was a meeting to transact the ' business of the society, and Mr. Widd was expelled and he thought he had no right to be present.—Mr. Widd said Mr. Wightman was out of order in bringing forward something which was not in accordance with the rules. Did Mr. Wightman think that it was right to ride over the rules and bring something forward at al meeting which was only a simple sJloscription meeting.l —Mr. Wightman said they had not come there to dis- cuss the matter and Mr. Widd would oblige them by rc- ' tiring.—Mr. Hewitt said it was not a summons meeting and they could not expel a member at such meeting.— Mr. Wightman moved that Mr. Widd and Mr. S. Dickens be asked to leave the room.—Mr. F. Hallett seconded. seconded.—Mr. Sharpe moved an amendment that they remain, as they were fully entitled members.—The mo- tion was put and 3 votes were recorded, and for the amendment s.—Mr. Widd said he was quite willing to leave the room, but he would like to say a few words. Mr. Wightman had charged him--Mr. Hallett (inter- rupting) said that was not the business and he objected to the matter being introduced.—The Chairman said the agenda stated the business was the passing of accounts and election of officers, and an appeal could be made by Mr. Widd to the committee and members in general in writing—Mr. Widd said he had made an appeal and sent it to The president of the club, who told him not to send any ap- peal,as Mr. Wies,htman's motion was out of order,as such business could only be transacted by a special meeting. —The Chairman said all he could do was to carry on the meeting in accordance with the agenda placed before him—The two members then left the room.—At this juncture Mr. A. Smith, the president,entered the room. On taking the chair,Mr. Smith said he was sorry to hear that the Society was passing through a crisis in its his- tory. He understood that Mr. Widd and Mr. Dickens had been in that room and ordered out. Ile did not know whether that was done deliberately or not.—Mr. Wightman : It has.—Mr. Smith said if that was so it was absolutely irregular. They were bound by the rules and if any person was to be expelled by the Society they must be expelled according to the ruhs. He read rule 21,and said a special meeting to which every member of the Society was summoned. No such meeting had been held,and it was irregular for Mr. Widd and Mr. Dickens to be expelled from the Seciety,and any person ordering them out did an illegal act, and put himself in a diffi- cult position, and he thought the two gentlemen namel should be invited to cOme into the room again. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Wightman said he strongly objected to sit at the same table as men who had done such as they had done.—Mr. Smith said if the society was to be carried on as they were carrying it on, he should retire from the claair and from the society, and they could please them- selves what they did. If everybody was not to have even handed justice in accordance will the rules, he must leave them to govern the society in their own way.— Mr. Wightman said if the chairman had properly looked after these men these things would not have happened. —The Chairman took that observation as a direct insult. He was not a servant of that society, and it was not his duty to go and root into the past title and past history of the birds in the show, and he must resent that he was in the smallest degree responsible for the exhibits in the show.—Mr. Wightman said the Chairman must have misunderstood his observation. What he meant was if the chairman had attended the meetings a little oftener these men would not have dared to do what they had done.—The Chairman said ho could understand plain English, and unless what had been said was withdrawn he should leave the chair.—Mr. Wightman said he would withdraw the statement and also his motion that Messrs. Widd and Dickens be ex- pmlled.—This was accepted and the two members were readmitted.—Tne balance sheet was then read and adopted, and the election of offi:ers resulted as follows : —President, Mr. A. Smith ; vic•-presideats. Rev. T. R. Matthews, Mr. F. Harwood and Mr. F. J. Wood ; secre- tary, Mr. W. Bray; treasurer, Mr. J. Lythgoe. A com- mittee was appointed.

DE GR EEF.

In view of the forthcoming visit to Warrington of Mons. de Greef, as announced in our advertising columns, the following biographical sketch of the distinguished pianist's career will be read with in- terest by a large section of the musical public. De Greef was born at Louvain,in Belgium,October 10th, 1862. From his earliest childhood he evinced great talent for music, and received a most careful liter- aryaud artistic education. Having carried off the priz- for piano at the Academy of his native town when he was nine years old, he went to the Con- servatoire at Brussels, where he studied piano. playing under Brassin, and harmony under Gevaert, tne well-known Principal of the School. At fifteen years of age he had carried off the first prize. Two years later he presented himself as a candidate to obtain the" Diplclme de Capacite," and he passed this examination with the greatest credit. Never before had the diploma been taken by a pianist. At this time De Greef made the acquaintance of Franz Liszt, who showed him the greatest sympathy, dedicated one of his works to him, and made him a present of an unedited manu- script. Meanwhile Da Greef,while still carrying on his course of studies in piano and harmony, com- menced at the Conservatoire, went on tour in Bel- gium, Germany, France, and Italy. He met with success everywhere, and his fame spread through- out his country to foreign countries. On December 11th,1887, by Royal command, he was called" Pro- fessor Superieur de Plane" at the Royal Conserva- toire of Brussels. De Greef wishing to make a pro- found study of the classic masters, learnt the harpsichord and the organ,and on his various tours he played the ancient compositions on the instru- ments for which they had originally been written. His sympathy with Edward Grieg attracted him to the North, and in the latter part part of 1888 he went for a tour in Norway with his friend af. Alexander Bull, son of the celebrated Norwegian violinist, Ole Bull. At Bergen he became aquainted with Griez, who declared him to be the finest inter- preter of his works that he had ever met. De Greef met with such great success in Norway. that he visited it again the following year, where he met with triumph after triumph, Oa his departure a concert was arranged(by telegraph) at Christiania ; all the musical people of the town were present. They covered the artist with flowers and greeted him with bravos, and as the steamer left, fireworks were sent off in his honour. A large crowd saw him off to the boat, and he was showered with " hurrahs " and cries of " revoir The traditions of the Conservatoire of Brussels are such that they do not allow the compositions of modern composers to be played ; Gevaert, however, broke through these traditions, and played a ballad by De Greef with his incomparable orchestra, in- terspersed with variations from an old popular song. This performance was so decidedly success- ful that it was repeated at the Brussels Conserva- toire, and also in the artistic circles of that town and at various other concerts. Dr. Abraham, head of the celebrated firm of Peters, in Leipzig, having o In p o thes it i ocno, hameaorndgthhisisrezmoiairekcatibolne. placedlBB9 which Grieg directed in Brussels, Da GrePf played concerts it the Concerto of this master with unparalleled suc- cos, At Paris, again, Grieg chose De Greef to inter- pret his works.

AARINUTON v": MO ItECANIBE.

-- A POOR GAME. If there are any certainties in football, the game between Warriugton and Morecambe at Warrington on Saturday, might have been considered a certain win for the homesterF. Not only are Morecambe the wooden•spoonists of two successive years but they were handsomely beaten on their own ground by Warrington earlier in the season. As the result shows, the homesters were justified in anticipating it win. Ten points to two is a fair margin, though less than at Morecambe. The visitors were well re- presented, while Warrington in addition to still de- ploring the absence of Bate and Barber, thought fit, to give Boscow a rest, and his place was taken by Jones cf the" A "team. Cross made a welcome re-appearance in the three-quarter line. The day was tine, and well-suited for a pleasant game. There was a noticeable falling off in the size of the gate, about 4,000 being the aggregate. Mr. Bes- I wick of Swinton, acted as referee, and the teams were composed as follows ; Morecambe : Elkin, full back ; U. lladwen, Wilson. Holmes, and Blacow,threequarters ; J.Had- wen and Armistead, halt backs ; T. Wilson, Cornth- waite, Shaw, Armistead, Chapman, Edmondson, Jackson, and Davies, forwards. Warringt,:m ; Jones, full back; Moores, Cross,Hal- , lam. and Isherwood,threequarters • Duckworth and Scott, half backs ; Taylor,Deakin,liarratt,Hill,Swift, R)by, Fairhurst, and Mason, forwards. Taylor set the ball in motion, and no return was made. Morecambe carried the first scrimmage, and Armistead punted down the field. Jones making a age return to centre. Scott and Cross got away, but the latter was tackled promptly. A judicious cross kick to Holmes saw the sea side 'crack" snap the ball up and .go off at a great pace. Jones ran him in touch, however, before he got far. For some time Morecambe's superior weight forward stood them in goad stead and they mere than held their own. They had got a good foot- ing in the Warrington half when Hallam relieved with a strong punt. Scott followed suit and the home front rank entered the Morecquabe half. Scott, Cross, and Tskerwood passed well, but the latter had no chance with Hadwen an Wilson. Holmes gave his side the advantage by a long kick, Jones being , pulled in 'touch. The visitors played with com- mendable dash and Jones was called upon to defend his line. Holmes made a fine opening for Wilson but the latter clumsily kicked dead. Over eager- ness to clear their line caused Warrington to be penalised and Blacow kicked an easy goal. The homesters finding themselves an unexpected minority wakened up at once and after repelling the invasion gave Morcambe some trouble. T,:e forwards made a gran' dribble half the length of the field,and Scott coming in at the finish kicked over the line and scored. Taylor failed at goal. The hemes'Ars now led by a point, but play still cmtinued to be slow and unscientific. Neither sets or backs combined well,though More- cambe were the worst. Hallam and Holmes were repeatedly prominent. The latter again got finely away aid after a long run handed to Wilson who beaded for the line with no one in front. A try seemed certain when Isherwood overtook the burly seasider and dropping at his feat checked him within 10 yards of the line, Warrington attacked again and Elkin was in difficulties, but saved well. Holmes led another incursion into the home half. Duckworth salely accounted for IL Hadwen, and several penalties against Morecambe spoiled their efforts to scire. Holmes was well watched, Moores bringing him down in close proximity to the line. Mason headed another ion; drioble which was stopped by Elkin in the corner,and an even spell of played ended in the score at half time by Warring- ton one try (three points), Morecambe one goal (two points). Corntliwaite started again and the visitors pressed at once, but only for a short time. Isherwood and Cross transferred play to the Morecambe line in a clever fashion, Elkin saving. When near the centre Holmes folowed up a punt and distancing the home team had a glorious chance but kicked too readily at the ball and List it in touch. This escape put Warrington on their mettle, and the forwards com- bining to more effect, Duckworth and Scott opened play out better. Moores and Mason made a speedy burst, Isherwood receiving the final pass only to be pulled down near the line. I iallam threw a chance away right on the line, and the next minute had hard luck with a drop at goal. A smart attack by Warrington ended in Moores just getting in but the try was mysteriously dis- allowed. Then Isherwood was robbed in HAI+ oppo- site corner, and when the score looked as if it was never coming Cross dropped a very neat goal from Hallam's pass. Warrington had nearly all the play though Holmes became dangerous through inter- cepting pass. Tay'or secured from the loose and handing to Mason that speedy forward scored be- hind the posts, Cross kicking another goal. Blacow changed to half back, but could afford no relief, though the visitors' defence was still good. 11. Hadwen ran well, but was soon grassed,and beyond a brief rush or two Warrington had little to defend themselves against. UREAT SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL

A meeting of this Council was held in thzi Board Schools, Great Sankey:on Monday evening. Mr. Thomas Halton presided, aud there were also present —Mr. G. Thompson, Mr. F. Kilshaw, Mr. Cooke, and Mr. J. Smith (clerk).

THE CANAL BRIDGE AT SANKEY BRIDGES. The Clerk read the copy of the letter he had forwarded to Mr. Stephenson, of the London and North Western Railway Company, with reference to the bridge over the canal at Sankey Bridges. He bad received the follow- ing reply from Mr. Harrison, the chief manager of the , Company :—"Our Engineer referred to me your letter of the 25th of September last, and I have since carefully considered the subject of it. lam assured that when- ever the maiu road bridge over the Ss. Helens Canal at Sankey Bridges is turned off for boat ttaffic, the relief bridge close to, is brought into use. Although the situa- tion of this relief bridge renders it necessary, as you state, to turn to it at right angles, it is a perfectly safe land convenient method of crossing the canal, but to save themselves a little trouble, drivers prefer to wait until the main road bridge has been replaced in its nor- mal position. I cannot admit, and I hardly think you will conteod,that this is a legitimate reason for objecting to the existing arrangements and certainly affords no ground for proposing the erection of a new bridge. The man in charge ef the bridge has received no complaint either from foot paasengers or anyone else, and if ob- struction does at any time occar it is due to the unao- conamodating character of the drivers themselves, and not to the insufficiency of the means of crossing the canal."—Tne Chairman : It is very evident that Mr. Harrison dces not know the true facts. The second bridge is always left in its normal position.—Mr.Cooke I prefer to wait when I ern taking a big load across than to go over the relief bridge. It is not safe.—The Chairman said the two bridges were so near together that it was impossible for the relief bridge to be turned when a boat was passing in the direction ef St. Helens The relief bridge is only used when the other bridge is being repaired or when a block takes place.—The Chairman : No driver with a big load on would risk erossing the other bridge. Right opposite the bridge there are some cottages, and there is very little room to turn round. The facts of the case cannot have been accurately stated to the manager.— The Clerk said he understood that a representative of the company had visited the bridge, and it was a pity that the Council was not informed of his coming be- cause they could have appointed a deputation to meet him, and explain the difficulties met with.—The Chair- man said when an accident occurred at the bridge it would be a serious matter.—The Clerk raid he believed one accident which occurred there cost the London and North-Western Railway Company over £l,OOO. He was afraid the Council could go no further in the matter. They might, however, endeavour to get a better bridge when the present one was to be replaced at some future date.—The Chairman said he could safely say that there were thousands of tons going • over the bridge every week.—The Clerk said the County Council were making an application for an Act of Parliament empowering them to contribute to the maintenance and repair of better bridges and the bridge under considera- tion seemed to be one of those cases it was intended to meet. If they wrote to Mr. Hutton, tolling him of the circumstances of the case, it might help him in his ap- plication. The Railway Company had provided a bridge which just met the requirements of the Act of Pattie- ment,and declined to do anything further.—Mr. Thomp- son said he was more concerned about the safety of the people who had to cross the bridge. Children going to school were in danger of being run over owing to the non-provision of a foot path.—Mr. Halton said it was also a hardship on horse-owners. He had seen as many ' as six or seven waiting on one side, and a dozen on the other, for a boat to paas.—Mr. Cook proposed, and Mr. Kilshaw seconded that the Clerk write to Mr. Hutton, clerk to the Coenty Council, on the matter.

TIER MORTUARY 4trESTION. The Clerk stated that he wrote to Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, calling his attention to the difficulties which had arisen with reference to the provision of a in utuary for Great Sankey in consequence of the audi- tor threatening to surcharge them with the amount pro- posed to be paid for a room for the reception of dead bodies. He asked for Mr. Brighouse's opinion on the matter, and had received the following reply :—" 23rd December. 1897. Dear Sir,—Your letter to Mr. Brig- house of 29:h November shall have his attention early in the new year. I have found the books bearing on the subject,but he has been so busy for the last month that he has not had time to tackle it. Don't think the matter has been forgotten. —Yours truly, GEORGR CARR."—The Chairman moved that pending a settlement of the ques- tion all dead bodies found in the parish be taken to the Chapel House Inn. The place in which the last body was put was unfit for the purpose. It was impossible to fasten the place up, and even children entered the place and saw the body.—The proposition was carried.

THE PARISI DOLL It communication from the Charity Commissioners was read with reference to the parish dole which was distributed by the Council. It was pointed out by the Commissioners that it was necessary for the Council to appoint two trustees to take charge of the charity, one to be appointed to serve two years, and another to serve four years.—Mr. Cooke said the dole caused more bother than it was worth.—lt was ultimately decided to appoint Mr. Halton and Mr. Thompson as trustees.

A DANGIIIIOII3 BEAM In accordance with notice given at the last meeting, Mr. Thompson moved a resolution with reference to the fence apposite Penketh•lann ends. He said it belonged to Lord Lilford, and he would propose that the Clerk write to the agent, drawing his attention to the dan- gerous state of the fencs. If the agent preferred to meet a deputation. two members of the Council would be pleased to meet him.—The Clerk thought the Highway Bosrd's Surveyor might have hie attention drawn to the matter.—The Chairman said the naatter was mentioned at a meeting of the Highway Board, and the Surveyor said the land belonged to Lord Lilford.—Mr. Cooke second^cl the proposition which was carried.

THE LIGHTING OF VEHICLES. A. communication was read from the Whiston Pariah Council asking the Council to petition the Lancashire County Council in favour of an order making it com- pulsory for owners of vehicles to carry lights after sun. set.—Mr. Thompson said ho was very much in favour of such an order. Bethought it ought to be compulsory for lights to be carried.—The Chairman said he was of the slime opinion.—Mr. Cooke proposed that the com- munication lie on the table. It was very awkward for farmers to fix lights to their vehicles ; especially when carting hay, straw, or other inflammable material.— Mr. Kilshaw seconded.—The Clerk : The Chairman will have the casting vote.—The Chairman thought it would be better to adjourn the matter until the next meeting when there would probably be a larger attendance.— This course was adopted. FAR:MONTE GRAIIIWZ SCHOOL. The Cl ult called the attention of the Council to the fact that two free scholarships tenable for three years were awarded each year after competitive examinations for Fern worth Grammar Schcol to boys over ten and under thirteen years of ago. Any boy in Great Sankey was entitled to compete in the examination. Mr. Clarke the schoolmaster, might know if be had any boys who would be likely to succeed.—The Chairman said the travelling expenses to Farnworth would be rather heavy for a parent. —Mr. Thompson said if a parent had a child likely to get on he would snake a sacrifice to en- able Gls child to do so.—Mr. Cooke suggested that Mr. Clarke should see if he had any boys likely to qualify.— It was decided that the Clerk should communicate with Mr. Clarke, the headmaster of the schools, on the sub- ject.

The ANNUAL MERTING OF THS COUNCIL. The Clerk informed the members that he had received information with reference to the forthcoming election of councillors. The annual meeting of the Council would be held on the 14th of March,and at that meeting the Council would be elected. This concluded the business. CANE SUGAR. ON THE ENGLISH MARKET.

The following is an extract from "The D tily Gleaner," published in Kingston, Jamaica, W.I. The West Indies, as pointed out by Mr. E. X. Leon to our interviewer some while ago, have still some friends in England. Mr. Leon,it will 'op remembered, directed the attention of ow readers to a Liverpool firm of ware- honsemeo and retailers who made auger a speciality,and explained to its customers that the sugar from cane is more economical inasmuch as its superiority in sweeten- ing properties over the beet sugar more than balances the differince in price between the two sugars. Mr. Leon has uow submitted to us one of the card- board 41b. boxes In which this firm—Messrs. Cooper and Co., who it appears have warehouses in Glasgow,Liver- pool and London, and branches all over the Kingdom,— retails its loaf cane sugar. The sugar is put up in 41los and 71bs boxes and each box bears on one side of it the following—" This sugar is refined from pure cane only, and is therefore more wholesome than beet root sugar, and being sweeter and stronger, is more economical." On another side are the words—. Warranted free from beet root, chemicals,or other impurities," and on a third side " Pure cane granulated sugar, pure and wholesome, sweet and economical," with a picture of the 41bs cotton bag of "fine granulated pure cane sugar," upon which bag appears the legend This is a perfectly pure dry sugar, and is specially recommended for all preserving purposes, as well as table use."

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

A coupls of much needed points fell to Warrington on Saturday, Morecambe being the means whereby they were obtained. During the recent period in which War- rington have done so badly, the fixture with Morecambe has been looked forward to as a ray of light in the dark- ness. A win, in almost everyone's opinion,was a "dead cert," and though counting point; before they are won is like counting chickens before they are hatched—and a trifle more precarious—the temerity of the homesters was net punished with disappoiltment.

Morecambe, be it said, are a consistent club, and in this respect have a decided advantage over Warrington. At the present time however their consistency is a mat- ter as much for commiseration and lament as Warring- ton's delightful inconsistency. The seasiders have lost match after match with a regularity which must be a source of joy to the man of order and method—and cone up smiling on each following Saturday. There must be something in the Morecambe air, beside sand, which conduces to the formation of a sanguine temperament and a deep love of Rugby football. The team arrived at Warrington in full force including the " best full back in Lancashire." whose acquaintance I made at More- cambe earlier in the season. A large number of specta- tors—seven I think—accompanied the spoon-holders to Warrington.

This bodyguard of seven had been selected from candi- dates possessed of big voices and affection for their fel- lows highly developed. The latter qualification causes them to address tho members of their team by their Christian names,and the former to make the air resound with the said names. The homn crowd were repeatedly electrified by hoarse cries of Now Elerbert,""Aadam's got it," etc. etc., as oce or other of these worthies got within kicking distance of ball or man. Though thus endearingly appealed to, Adam, Herbert and Co., failed to achieve the doughty deeds imagined for them, and had to accept defeat by 10 points to 2.

All this preamble is not meant to prove that More- cambe cannot play football. They may have a style of their own, but they peg away in a determined fashion, and on Saturday at least their style was not one bit in- ferior to Warrington's method. It was a distinctly poor game from a scientific point of view. Combination ap- peared thrown to the winds, and the reason for this on the home side was the absence of Boscow, Bate, and Barber, the three busy B's who keep things going. The game opened to the disadvantage of Warrington. Their opponents used superior weight,and ideas which were all at sea—or from sea—in the scrimmages, and quickly forced their way to the line. A numiscr of clumsy efforts to alter this was made by the homesters, which ended in their being too previous and having a penalty imposed. A Morecambite calmly kicked a goal amid "told you so" looks from some of the despondent ones. An enquiry as to the kicker elicited the fact that it was Black Cow, and in feverish ha9te I turned to the bet of players, only to have the alluring mystery dispelled by the discovery that the murderer of his mother tongue meant Blacow.

This score was so unexpe—ct—ed by Warrington, who had anticipated nilling their opponents, that it acted like an elixir. The succeeding movements were all in their favour, and the forwards, whose superiority in the open was most merked,carried the ball to the other end with a clever dribble. Scott dropped on the ball as it crossed the line, and the goal kick failing, Warrington were a point ahead. From this stage to the interval there was not much to choose between th; teams, though the homesters were oftener dangerous. Holmes was a tower of strength to his side, and had the other backs been quicker they trius'; have scored from till openings he made. Isherwood effected ene daring save in a magnificent manner, and the teams crossed over separated by a point. The second half was nearly all in favour of Warrington. It was a wonder how the backs were kept out, and at last Cross increased the lead with a clever dropped goal. Very soon after Mason credited himself with a try,Pross adding a second goal. The rest of the game was spent by the Warrington backs making fruitless attempts to score, the monotony of tne attack being varied by a clever dribble from Elolmes,who must have scored but for kicking in touch.

On the Warrington side there is a little credit for any of the backs. umnbination was so badly lacking that in dividual efforts scarcely deserve mention. Moores,Cross Hallam and Isherwood all shone at times, but not with too great brilliancy. Duckworth and Scott were slower than usual, and Jones will need more practice in taking the ball on the run before he can relieve Boscow. Taylor and Mason were the most prominent forwards in the loose, the latter especially showing resource and clever- ness. I think the Morecambe team played well for their reputation. They worked hard and were several times within an ace of scaring, luck being against them. Holmes played a better game than I have seen him do previously, and if he would do less zig-zagging and hop- ping about, would be far more dangeroue. Elkin was very safe at back, and H. Hadwen, Wilson, and Arms stead deserve praise far unremitting exertions.

The display of Warrington did not augur well for to- day's match. On such form as that Oldham would have run in half a dozen tries without difficulty. For- tunately Bate and Bascow are down to play again, and though Barber's sterling play will be much misced, those who saw what Warrington could do at Oldham with 14 men, are not a ithout hope. To lose here would practi- cally mean to lose the championship for Oldham, so that every effort is certain to be made. Without any ill- feeling to the clover exponents from Spindledom I should be well pleased to see last season's fine performance re- peated by Warrington. It may seem improbable, but determination will get over many difficulties,and a really desperate effort might carry Warrington to victory. Could Warrington just revenge themselves on Oluham and Salford on successive Saturdays, I for one would be prepared to overlook any failings for the rest of the season.

Widnes nun their masters on Saturday, and a win by 13 points to 5 with 14 men is one of Oldham's finest per- formances so far. The game was, however, a particu- larly poor one, and there was an element of roughness and ill-feeling about the proceedings which reflected discredit upon both teama alike. With Swinton also gaining a couple of points at St. Helens, the relative position of the two leaders remains unchanged,the"dark blues" being four points behind with a couple of matches in hand. On the theory that a bird in the hand is worth two in the bush, Oldham have of course the best of the argument, but the Swintonians are showing such sterl- ing farm that it is difficult to see who will talcs their number down unless it be tht leaders themselves, and the meeting n I the two on February 19 is already being eagerly anticipated.

With Salford unexpectedly going down before Tyldes- ler, Broughton Rangers:advance into fourth place with 22 points out of 16 matches played. Wigan had all their work cut out to deteat Stockport, only a dbuple of points separating the teams at the close. Runcorn are dropping lower and lower almost every week, and the club is in- deed the victim of dire mi.fortune. Leigh are the latest to capture points out of the first season's ehampions,and but for the magnificent play of Houghton it is bard to say what huge scores would be run up against the Cheshire men.

The " A "team covered itself with glory alai by re- turning from Los ock Graham with a victory of 13 points to all. Carey was the hero of the match, scoring all three tries. Whatever his faulte may be when tried for the premier team, the sturdy little Irishman is going great guns amongst smaller fry. Hamm kicked a couple of goals, and the score was just about a correct indication a the play. --- LANCASHIRE (NORTHERN UNION) SENIOR COMPETITION.

WARRINGTON LITERARY AND PHILO.

SOPHICAL SOCIETY.

LECTURE BY MR. S. P. SIE,COCK, F.R 1.8. A. The fourth meeting of the above named society for the session was held in the Masonic Rooms, Bold-st,eet, on Monday evening, the chair being taken by the President. Mr. J. Rymer Young, F.C.S. There was a good attend- ance. The PRESIDENT, in opening the proceedings, remarked that that was not the first occasion they had been ad-- dressed on architectural matters. Six or seven years; ago Mr. William Owen gave them a charming lecture on "Chester Cathedral," and two years ago Professor Simp- son, of Liverpool, a paper on " The Architecture of Old. Liverpool ;" but the subject Mr. Silcock had selected had not been previously lectured upon before the society. It had been said that modesty was one of the attributeeeof true greatness ; if so, it was a q iality which the junior' members of the society seemed to posese in a conspicu- ous degree, and it had taken him nearly three years to persuade Mr. Silcock that it was his duty to give them, a paper on some architectural subject. He called upon him to address them. (Applause.) Mr. S. P. SILCOCL, F.R.l.B.A.,then read an interesting paper on " " Three Famous Domes : Their history and, construction," with illustrations, the lantern being skil- fully manipulated by Mr. H. N.Houghton. The lecturer stated that he regretted that while so much had been, written on the history of the pointed arch,so little should have been said regarding the history of domes, the one being a mere constructive peculiarity tnat might have been dispensed with, and the other the noblest feature in the styles in which it prevailed, and, perhaps, the most important acquisition with which science had en- riched the art of architecture. The lecturer then pro- ceeded to refer in detail to the three domes he had selected for discussion, namely, the Pantheon at Rome. St. Peter's at Rome, and St. Paul's, London. He stated that he would endeavour to Show the gradual develop- ment of the three domes,but it would be well to enumer- ate the points of difference, and to render his remarks clearer,he had had two photographs prepared of each of the three domes, the first showing the plans and the second the sections, all drawn eat to one uniform scale. The first point to notice was the supporting area. The Pantheon had its round wall over 19 feet thick, but that was not solid throughout. St Peter's had its four piers, forming the square, with an arch face nearly 29 feet thick. St. Paul's had its eight piers forming the octa- gon. The dome of the Pantheon sprang from the CU". cular wafllrsomof thf,lßroaturenhdeas, the St .Peter's ediarduernn tioyfeSs aspnrgalensg. The drum of St. Paul's sprang from eight arches, but without pendentives. The diameter of the Pantheon was 1451 t. Sin., the diameter of S. Peter's was 140 ft., and that of St. Paul's, at the Whispering Gallery was 112 ft. The height to the eve of the Pan- theon was 147 ft.. and to the eye of the dome of St. Peter's 3301 t. The height to the eye of the inner demo of St. Paul's was 216 ft. and to the eye of the cone 266 ft. The dome of the Pantheon was one solid cover- ing, and the dome of St. Peter's was in two castings for the most part. 'f he dome of St. Paul's had one support- ing cone with an inner and out dome for appearance. The thrust of the Pantheon was resisted principally by means of weight of solid construction placed upon the haunches. The thrust of the dome of St. Peter's was broken up and made into actual weight by means of the diminishing ribs (a sketch of which was shown), where- as in St. Paul's the energy of the thrust was counter- acted by a thicknessine of materiel at the haunches very considerably aided by encircling chains. Abutment was also obtained in the two latter domes by the colonading placed round the drums. The Pantheon was lighted by one centre eve, whereas the domes of St. Peters and St. Paul's were lighted by windows placed round the drums. The Pantheon was probably built some time between the period of Hadrian and Constantine, say A.D. 250., but that date was uncertain. St. Peter's was commenced in 1508, and the forecourt was completed in 1661, tak- ing practically 155 years to build. St. Paul's was com- menced in 1675, and the top gene of the lantern was put on in 1710, being 35 years. (Applause.) The PRESIDENT remarked that it was evident Mr. Sit- cock had given an enormous amount of time to the subject, and also that he had tem put to considerable trouble to give them such a lecture. (Hear, hear.) Mr. CRARLES WRITE, in proposing a vote of thanks 10 Bee. Silcock,observed that the lecture had raised their re- spect and admiration for the men of genius who could conceive and construct such magnificent buildings as those which had been brought under their attention that evening. What was the use of the dome ? Was it only for ornament,or was there some inner meaning in hav- ing such enormous spaces of air ? Was there a sanitary idea about it ? Captain EDELSTEN seconded the motion; and said that when he heard that Mr. Silcock would speak upon one of his own subjects he knew it would be treated with skill and care, and he had not in any sense been disap- pointed. The dome was meanineless,and without soul, and that was what struck them when they regarded it from what Ruskin called " The artistic or rational point of admiration." It was a matter of wonder that the dome had not been more used for public municipal buildings. There was,bowever,a noble example in the Capitol at Washington, but there was no similar exam- ple in England. He would confine the use of the dome to purely utiliterian needs and deny it a place amongst the higher conceptions of architecture. (Applause.) Mr. THOP.IAS MAY remarked that tho (1,..me might be said to be the material counterpart or structural imitae tion of the rainbow. There were great advantages in the structure of the dome.

The resolution was then put to the meeting and heartily agreed to, and a vote of thanks having also been accorded to Mr. Houghton for his service at the lantern.

Mr. Sucocir, in replying said, it was a fact that Sir Christopher Wren did not want to build his dome nearly el high as it was erected, but in the old St. Paul's there was a high tower and spire, and the cry was for some- thing to take their place. Probably the origin of the dome was the wish to eupply the place of the tower and spire. It was, after all, a question of taste. Personally he pre- ferred the classicalto the gothic, and the dome, in his opinion, superseded the tower and the spire, but per- haps not from an artiatic and se3thetic point of view.

particular, and neitner or &nem suirect. tine was affected in her speech. The other had a strong Scottish accent. So you see, Mrs. Park, that it depends entirely upon his caprice whether he engages you or not. If you can bear his tem- per, and he should be pleased with you, he will pay you liberally." " Is gastric fever a dangerous disease ?" asked Beryl. "As in all fevers, people die of it, but I don't think my lord will die. He is a very giant for strength. His illness is to me most mysterious. Lie is temperate in his living and all his habits. People seem to have the impression that he is very ill. They were leaving cards all day yesterday, even when he was out. The medical man said this morning. that his lordship is not to see many visi- tors. I notice that people are paying court to Mr. and Mrs. Conyers assiduously now. Mr. Conyers is Lord Hawkhurst's heir," the housekeeper added, for Beryl's benefit. "And if Lord Hawkhurst dies Mr. Conyers will be the next earl, will he not ? " asked BeryL "Yea. Mr. Conyers is a distant relative, but the nearest in orter o succession to_the title. The property is not like most noblemen's estates; it Is not entailed, and if my lord were to die without a will it would be divided equally between Mr. Conyers and Mr. Desmond, who have equal claims." Beryl started. "Mr. Desmond?' said Miss Cavanagh. "Who is he 7" "A young kinsman of my lord. He is Noble Desmond, a young barrister." "Ah !" muttered Miss Cavanagh. " She's in the very nest of 'em, sure. The earl and Conyers and Desmond are all one tribe, anyhow, and that's curious." "I didn't understand," said Mrs. Dominick. " Were you asking further about young Mr. Des- mond? Don't you know the family history? The family name of the earls of Hawlthurst is Desmond. When my lord was a young man and before he came into the title, he was Lord Gray Desmond." " Gray I" murmured Beryl. "Was he ever a colonel in the army ?" "No ; his father was earl, and he was the heir. He had no need of any profession," said Mrs. Dominick. "Mr. Conyers's father was a Des- mond, but changed his name to Conyers on marrying an heiress of that name. My lord is a widower and childless, Mrs. Park, but he's young still, being not over fifty years of age. Mr. Conyers will inherit everything at my lord's death, except a trifling annuity to Mr. Desmond, I suppose." At this moment, a bell rang sharply close at hand. "My lord's bell," said Mrs. Dominick, rising. "He is calling for me, He is in the library. Come with me, Mrs. Park, and I will pre,ent you to his lordship. Bridget Cavanagh, just wait until I come back to you. Keep up your courage, Mrs. Park; dont get frightened; there's no knowing but that his lordship may engage you. I'll give you a warm recommendation, for my cousin's sake. Come !" She led the way into the hall and up-stairs to Lord Hawkhurst's library. To be continued). •
